## ﻿**Artificial intelligence in endoscopy and colonoscopy: a comprehensive bibliometric analysis of global research trends
**Core ML Technology:** Artificial intelligence technologies encompassing machine learning and deep learning algorithms, specifically Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs), Deep Convolutional Neural Networks (DCNNs), and Computer-Aided Diagnosis (CAD) systems for real-time image analysis.

## 1. The "Big Picture" & Motivation
* What is the main problem your research is trying to solve?
The research aims to conduct a comprehensive bibliometric and visualization analysis of global research focused on AI applications in endoscopy and colonoscopy. It addresses the need to understand how AI is enhancing the detection and characterization of gastrointestinal lesions (like polyps and neoplasms), which traditionally suffer from operator-dependent variability, varying detection rates, and missed lesions.
* What made you choose this specific topic?
The advent of AI in medical diagnostics has markedly transformed gastroenterology. A comprehensive bibliometric analysis is particularly valuable due to the rapid expansion, interdisciplinary nature, and global interest surrounding AI-driven medical technologies. Traditional literature reviews often fail to fully capture these complex, evolving thematic trends and collaboration networks.
* Why does this research matter for the industry right now?
AI is actively being integrated into clinical practice to provide real-time image analysis and decision support. This standardizes examinations, improves diagnostic accuracy, reduces the incidence of missed lesions, and optimizes procedural efficiency. The study provides data-driven insights necessary for guiding future research investments, policy decisions, and clinical practice advancements in AI-enhanced gastroenterology.

## 2. The Practical Approach (Methodology)
* Are you doing mostly lab experiments, data analysis, or fieldwork?
Data analysis. The study is a systematic bibliometric analysis of published scientific literature.
* What framework or formulas are you using to analyze things?
The data were analyzed using visualization tools like VOSviewer, CiteSpace, and the R Bibliometrix package (Biblioshiny). Analytical frameworks included co-authorship analysis, co-citation analysis, bibliographic coupling, keyword co-occurrence, and cluster analysis (utilizing log-likelihood ratio tests, modularity, and silhouette scores). Time-slicing with Kleinberg's burst detection algorithm was also used to identify sudden spikes in citation activity.
* How do you usually collect your data or samples?
A systematic search was conducted in September 2024 within the Web of Science Core Collection. The dataset was collected by querying for original research and review articles focused on AI applications in endoscopy and colonoscopy, using keywords like "Endoscopy," "Colonoscopy," "Artificial Intelligence," and "Machine Learning."

## 3. Obstacles & Breakthroughs
* What is the biggest challenge you've faced with this project?
The analysis was confined to English-language publications indexed in the Web of Science Core Collection, potentially introducing geographic bias and overlooking relevant research in other languages or databases. Additionally, citation-based metrics can lag for newer studies. A broader challenge for the field itself is standardizing AI algorithms across diverse populations, equipment, and workflows to ensure global generalizability.
* Have you hit any dead ends or unexpected results yet?
The study noted a slight decline in publications in 2024. However, it was clarified that this was an artifact of the data collection cutoff (September 2024) representing an incomplete year, rather than an actual reduction in research activity or interest.
* What has been your favorite finding or "eureka" moment?
The identification of key emerging research clusters centered around "adenoma detection," "polyp segmentation," and "wireless capsule endoscopy," reflecting the field's shift toward real-time diagnostic improvements. Furthermore, the meta-analyses highlighted that AI significantly improves adenoma detection rates (ADR) by 50% without increasing withdrawal time, showing its independence from conventional human perceptual constraints.

## 4. Lit Review & The "Gap"
* What major gap did you find in the previous research?
Previous traditional systematic reviews fail to offer a visually interpretable and data-driven overview of the expanding scientific landscape. In terms of the field itself, there is a lack of clear international standards or regulatory guidance for AI-driven clinical accountability, as well as an under-exploration of disparities in AI access across low- and middle-income countries.
* Who is the go-to author or foundation paper in your field?
Mori Y emerged as the leading author by publication volume, and Tada T as the most cited author. Foundational milestone papers include Wang et al. (2019, Gut) on AI increasing adenoma detection rates, Hirasawa et al. (2018, Gastric Cancer) on CNNs for gastric cancer detection, and Urban et al. (2018, Gastroenterology) on deep learning for real-time polyp localization.
* Did you find any major contradictions between previous studies?
No major contradictions were highlighted in the bibliometric findings. However, a terminological discrepancy was noted: the explicit keyword "Artificial Intelligence" appeared with surprisingly low frequency (only ~4% of occurrences), which is explained by the literature using more specific ML terminology like "neural network," "classification," or "machine learning."

## 5. Future Impact & Advice
* What do you think is the next step for this research area?
Future bibliometric research should leverage large language models (LLMs) to incorporate and analyze non-English literature to overcome geographic bias. Additionally, future analyses should specifically examine variations in clinical adoption, practical challenges, regulatory environments, cost-effectiveness, and healthcare infrastructure that influence AI implementation globally.
* Where do you see your findings being applied in real life?
The bibliometric findings will help researchers, clinicians, and policymakers understand current gaps and trends to guide future investments. The underlying AI technologies are directly applied in real-time colonoscopy and endoscopy workflows to distinguish benign from malignant lesions, ultimately facilitating early intervention for colorectal and gastric cancers.
* Any tips for someone trying to read papers in this domain?
When searching the literature, look for specific AI techniques and diagnostic terms (e.g., "neural network," "machine learning," "polyp segmentation") rather than relying solely on the broad term "Artificial Intelligence." Focus on highly cited foundational papers and multicentric randomized trials to understand the transition from proof-of-concept algorithms to practice-ready, real-time clinical tools.

## Is the use of artificial intelligence the main stage for detecting polyps during colonoscopy? (DOI: 10.3748/wjg.v31.i22.106500)
**Core ML Technology:** Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs) for endoscopic polyp detection and classification (Computer-Aided Detection / CAD), alongside machine learning algorithms including XGBoost (Extreme Gradient Boosting) and multivariate logistic regression for risk factor prediction and post-polypectomy recurrence surveillance.

## 1. The "Big Picture" & Motivation
* What is the main problem your research is trying to solve?
Colorectal cancer (CRC) is the third most frequently diagnosed cancer and the second leading cause of cancer death worldwide. While screening colonoscopy and the timely detection and resection of colorectal polyps are critical for prevention, interpreting endoscopic visual information (polyp size, morphology, location, histological subtype) is complex and subject to human error. The research addresses whether artificial intelligence (AI) should serve as the primary ("main stage") driver in polyp detection and surveillance, while evaluating the technical, clinical, and methodological hurdles associated with AI deployment in colonoscopy.

* What made you choose this specific topic?
The surging interest and debate surrounding AI in gastroenterology, particularly the integration of computer-aided detection (CAD) systems in colonoscopy and recent breakthroughs such as the work by Shi et al., which utilized machine learning models (XGBoost) for one-year post-polypectomy surveillance and recurrence prediction.

* Why does this research matter for the industry right now?
Although AI tools offer promising gains in polyp detection rates, rapid adoption without adequate standardization carries risks of elevated false-positive diagnoses, extended procedure times, endoscopist cognitive distraction, unnecessary polypectomies, and potential operator deskilling. Clarifying AI's optimal role is vital for developing sound clinical guidelines and maximizing healthcare cost-effectiveness.

## 2. The Practical Approach (Methodology)
* Are you doing mostly lab experiments, data analysis, or fieldwork?
The work is a critical review, comparative synthesis, and methodological evaluation of clinical trial data, retrospective cohort analyses (e.g., Shi et al.'s 1,694-patient polypectomy dataset, Knudsen et al.'s 195,453-patient 12-year screening cohort), and deep learning image classification experiments (e.g., Ozawa et al.'s dataset of 20,431 images).

* What framework or formulas are you using to analyze things?
The analysis synthesizes:
1. Supervised machine learning algorithms—specifically XGBoost (Extreme Gradient Boosting) and multivariate logistic regression—used to model independent prognostic variables for polyp recurrence.
2. Deep Convolutional Neural Network (CNN) architectures trained and validated on extensive endoscopic image datasets for spatial feature extraction and polyp classification.
3. Meta-analytic statistical frameworks pooling randomized controlled trials (RCTs) evaluating diagnostic error reduction metrics (e.g., P < 0.001) between computer-aided colonoscopy and standard white-light colonoscopy.

* How do you usually collect your data or samples?
Data is sourced from high-definition white-light and AI-assisted colonoscopy video streams and still image captures across multicenter patient cohorts. Datasets include endoscopic visual characteristics (polyp dimensions, location, morphology, Paris/pit pattern classifications), histopathological evaluations, and longitudinal patient health records (demographics, family history, smoking duration).

## 3. Obstacles & Breakthroughs
* What is the biggest challenge you've faced with this project?
Overcoming the fundamental disconnect between human synthetic perception and computer associative analysis. AI systems require highly standardized, pre-processed input structures, and training models on small patient cohorts risks parameter drift/overfitting and poor clinical generalizability.

* Have you hit any dead ends or unexpected results yet?
Yes. Computer-aided detection (CAD) has failed to demonstrate statistically significant increases in detecting advanced adenomas (> 10 mm), sessile serrated lesions, or invasive CRC in several clinical studies. Furthermore, CAD systems have introduced elevated false-positive rates, increased endoscopist visual reaction times, distracted practitioners from assessing normal mucosa, and lengthened procedure times.

* What has been your favorite finding or "eureka" moment?
The conceptual clarity that AI functions best not as an autonomous replacement for clinical judgment, but as a transformative, complementary decision-support tool. Additionally, the finding that ML models (like XGBoost) can effectively risk-stratify long-term polyp recurrence based on specific multivariate risk factors (smoking history, age, family history), complementing real-time visual detection.

## 4. Lit Review & The "Gap"
* What major gap did you find in the previous research?
Existing literature suffers from small-sample pilot studies lacking statistical power, wide regional disparities and limitations in surveillance guidelines, insufficient data standardization for video streaming inputs, and a disproportionate focus on initial polyp detection rather than longitudinal post-polypectomy recurrence and clinical outcome metrics.

* Who is the go-to author or foundation paper in your field?
Key foundation works include:
- Shi et al. (2025) for machine learning-based post-polypectomy surveillance modeling using XGBoost (World Journal of Gastroenterology).
- Knudsen et al. (2016) for the landmark 12-year longitudinal study of 195,453 individuals proving colonoscopy screening efficacy in reducing CRC incidence and mortality.
- Ozawa et al. (2020) for establishing robust CNN training on large-scale polyp and normal tissue endoscopic image databases.

* Did you find any major contradictions between previous studies?
Yes. Several meta-analyses of randomized trials show significant reductions in diagnostic error rates with AI assistance (P < 0.001), whereas other clinical trials report no improvement in the detection of advanced/serrated lesions, accompanied by increased false-positive rates, visual distraction, and potential endoscopist deskilling.

## 5. Future Impact & Advice
* What do you think is the next step for this research area?
Conducting large-scale, multi-center randomized controlled trials comparing AI-assisted colonoscopy against traditional colonoscopy across diverse data standardization methods; developing specialized pre-processed video streaming architectures tailored for AI associative recognition; and evaluating long-term patient health outcomes and operator competency.

* Where do you see your findings being applied in real life?
In next-generation intelligent endoscopy suites featuring real-time CADe/CADx visual guidance to assist endoscopists, alongside clinical decision-support systems integrated into hospital EHRs that utilize ML algorithms (e.g., XGBoost) to generate personalized post-polypectomy surveillance schedules.

* Any tips for someone trying to read papers in this domain?
Differentiate strictly between computer-aided detection (CADe), computer-aided diagnosis/characterization (CADx), and predictive surveillance modeling. Always critically assess the training dataset size, the clinical relevance of detected polyps (diminutive vs. advanced adenomas), the reported false-positive rates, and whether the study measures real-world patient outcomes or merely offline image benchmark accuracy.

## Introducing a Deep Neural Network Model with Practical Implementation for Polyp Detection in Colonoscopy Videos** (DOI: 10.4103/jmss.jmss_23_24)
**Core ML Technology:** Multi-task Deep Convolutional Neural Network utilizing Transfer Learning via an ImageNet-pretrained 50-layer ResNet50 backbone for feature extraction, coupled with dual three-layer feedforward neural network heads for simultaneous bounding box regression and binary polyp/non-polyp classification. Trained end-to-end using a weighted multi-task Mean Squared Error (MSE) loss function ($L_{total} = L_{reg} + \lambda L_{cls}$) with the Adam optimizer (learning rate = 0.001, batch size = 32, 20 epochs) in PyTorch.

## 1. The "Big Picture" & Motivation
* What is the main problem your research is trying to solve?
Colorectal cancer prevention heavily relies on surveillance colonoscopy to identify and resect adenomatous polyps before they progress into malignancy. However, there is an unacceptably high and variable rate of missed neoplastic lesions among endoscopists due to wide variations in polyp size, shape, color, texture, and visual similarity to surrounding healthy mucosa. While computer-aided detection (CADe) systems can assist clinicians, existing deep learning models (such as YOLO variants, R-CNN, and RetinaNet) are excessively complex, heavily parameterized, computationally costly, and prone to false positives due to the absence of negative (non-polyp) training examples, impeding practical deployment in standard clinical settings.

* What made you choose this specific topic?
The adenoma and polyp detection rate is directly correlated with reduced colorectal cancer mortality and serves as a key quality metric in gastrointestinal endoscopy. Existing commercial and research AI systems (e.g., EndoMind based on YOLOv5, GI Genius, Discovery) demand high-end GPU infrastructure, are difficult to modify or fine-tune, and suffer from heavy computational requirements. This motivated the design of a lightweight, resource-efficient, and accurate multi-task neural network capable of performing real-time polyp classification and bounding box localization on low-cost hardware.

* Why does this research matter for the industry right now?
Real-time clinical deployment requires fast frame-processing rates without the need for expensive, dedicated GPU server setups in every endoscopy suite. By achieving 100% classification accuracy, 86% bounding box detection accuracy, and an ultra-fast processing time of 0.01 seconds per frame (with compatibility for CPU-only execution), this architecture provides an accessible, cost-effective CADe solution that can be integrated into standard endoscopic towers to reduce missed lesion rates and improve diagnostic outcomes globally.

## 2. The Practical Approach (Methodology)
* Are you doing mostly lab experiments, data analysis, or fieldwork?
The study focuses on computational experiments and empirical data analysis. It entails dataset curation and negative-sample extraction from colonoscopy videos, multi-task convolutional neural network architectural design, transfer learning parameter fine-tuning, and systematic benchmarking against state-of-the-art models (YOLOv5 / EndoMind) and 5-fold cross-validation using the PyTorch framework on a testbench configured with an Intel Core i7-6700 3.4 GHz CPU, 32 GB RAM, and an NVIDIA GeForce GTX 1060 (6 GB) GPU.

* What framework or formulas are you using to analyze things?
The implementation is built in Python using PyTorch. The architecture uses a pretrained ResNet50 backbone (50 layers with residual shortcut connections) feeding into two parallel 3-layer feedforward heads. The model optimizes a joint weighted Mean Squared Error (MSE) loss function:
$$L_{total} = \frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^N \sum_j (y_{reg, j}^{(i)} - \hat{y}_{reg, j}^{(i)})^2 + \lambda \frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^N \sum_j (y_{cls, j}^{(i)} - \hat{y}_{cls, j}^{(i)})^2$$
where $\lambda$ balances the contribution of the regression and classification error terms during backpropagation. Training is performed using the Adam optimizer (learning rate = 0.001, batch size = 32, 20 iterations). Object localization performance is evaluated using Intersection over Union (IoU):
$$\text{IoU}(A, B) = \frac{\text{Area}(A \cap B)}{\text{Area}(A \cup B)}$$
with $\text{IoU} > 0.5$ defining True Positives (TP). Confusion matrices, precision, processing latency per frame, and 5-fold cross-validation are used for evaluation.

* How do you usually collect your data or samples?
Data was curated by combining positive polyp benchmark datasets with newly extracted negative (non-polyp) video frames:
1. **KVASIR-SEG:** 1,000 colonoscopy images with polyps, segmentation masks, and bounding box annotations (resolutions from 487×332 to 1072×1920 pixels).
2. **CVC-ClinicDB:** 612 colonoscopy images containing polyps and corresponding binary masks.
3. **LDPolypVideo dataset:** 1,586 clear, noise-free, blur-free, and specularity-free non-polyp frames extracted directly from colonoscopy video sequences to provide essential negative training controls.
All images were standardized and resized to $224 \times 224$ pixels. An 80/20 train/test split with stratified sampling was employed to ensure balanced class distributions per batch.

## 3. Obstacles & Breakthroughs
* What is the biggest challenge you've faced with this project?
The primary challenges included: (1) The severe lack of non-polyp images in public colonoscopy benchmarks, which historically caused models to produce excessive false positives on healthy tissue; (2) Balancing high detection accuracy with low architectural complexity to avoid the excessive memory footprints and GPU requirements of multi-stage detectors like Faster R-CNN and YOLO; and (3) Accommodating the wide variability in polyp geometry, texture, lighting artifacts, and partial occlusions during real-time video feeds.

* Have you hit any dead ends or unexpected results yet?
Initial training on polyp-only datasets prevented the calculation of True Negatives (TN) in the confusion matrix and caused the network to struggle when differentiating non-polyp mucosal surfaces. Furthermore, when evaluating against human specialist annotations, the authors encountered instances where expert labels were inaccurate or missed existing polyps, which initially registered as false-positive discrepancies.

* What has been your favorite finding or "eureka" moment?
The most notable breakthroughs were: (1) The proposed deep neural network was able to successfully detect subtle, hidden polyps that had been entirely overlooked or mislabeled by expert endoscopists in the ground-truth datasets; and (2) Achieving 100% binary classification accuracy (zero false positives and zero false negatives) alongside 86% bounding box detection at an inference speed of 0.01 seconds per frame (15.02 seconds total training time), matching YOLOv5 while maintaining a lightweight footprint capable of running on standard CPUs.

## 4. Lit Review & The "Gap"
* What major gap did you find in the previous research?
Prior research focused heavily on large, highly complex architectures (e.g., YOLOv3/v4/v5, DarkNet53, Faster R-CNN, RetinaNet, EfficientDet, PraNet, and DDANet) with immense parameter counts that necessitate high-end GPUs, making them impractical for low-cost, resource-constrained medical environments. Additionally, previous studies almost exclusively trained models on polyp-only images without sufficient non-polyp control frames, failing to adequately address false-positive rates in real-world continuous endoscopic video streams.

* Who is the go-to author or foundation paper in your field?
Key foundation references include:
- **Jha et al. (2020, 2021):** Creators of the Kvasir-SEG benchmark and ColonSegNet comparative evaluation framework.
- **Bernal et al. (2015):** Creators of the CVC-ClinicDB benchmark dataset.
- **Fan et al. (2020) & Pozdeev et al. (2023):** Developers of PraNet (reverse attention modules) and FCN/U-Net architectures for real-time segmentation.
- **He et al. (2016) / Ahamed et al. (2024):** Foundational residual learning framework establishing ResNet50 for transfer learning without vanishing gradients.
- **Commercial CADe Systems:** GI Genius, Discovery, EndoMind, Ai4Gi, and EndoBrain.

* Did you find any major contradictions between previous studies?
While prevailing literature suggested that achieving competitive polyp detection requires deep, complex architectures (such as 106-layer Darknet/YOLO, multi-scale feature pyramids FPN/PANet/BiFPN, or heavy regional proposal networks), this study proved that a streamlined 50-layer ResNet50 backbone combined with lightweight feedforward heads can match or outperform complex systems like YOLOv5/EndoMind without overfitting or requiring dedicated GPU accelerators. Additionally, the study highlighted that treating polyp detection solely as single-task bounding box regression without simultaneous negative-sample classification yields poor specificity in realistic continuous endoscopy streams.

## 5. Future Impact & Advice
* What do you think is the next step for this research area?
Future directions include expanding the training dataset with multi-center colonoscopy video streams from diverse camera manufacturers and patient populations, incorporating temporal tracking mechanisms across video frames to prevent bounding-box jitter, and conducting real-time embedded clinical trials on low-cost hardware in live hospital endoscopy rooms.

* Where do you see your findings being applied in real life?
Direct integration into commercial and open-source CADe modules within clinical endoscopy towers, video capture systems, and portable edge-computing hardware in hospitals and outpatient surgical centers. Because it operates efficiently even on central processing units (CPUs) with low memory usage, it enables widespread, affordable adoption in resource-limited clinics to reduce diagnostic error rates and assist trainee endoscopists.

* Any tips for someone trying to read papers in this domain?
1. **Scrutinize Dataset Composition:** Always check whether benchmark datasets include verified negative (non-polyp) frames, as models trained solely on positive images will exhibit high false-positive rates during live video endoscopy.
2. **Prioritize Efficiency vs. Complexity:** Evaluate whether reported accuracy gains justify the computational and latency costs, especially when targeting real-time (≥30–60 FPS) and CPU/edge deployment.
3. **Analyze Metric Definitions:** Look closely at IoU thresholds (e.g., IoU > 0.5), classification confusion matrices (TP, TN, FP, FN), and cross-validation protocols rather than relying solely on global accuracy figures, keeping in mind that human ground-truth annotations in medical vision can occasionally contain labeling oversights.

## The impact of artificial intelligence on the endoscopic assessment of inflammatory bowel disease-related neoplasia
**DOI: 10.1177/17562848251348574**
**Core ML Technology:** Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs; e.g., EfficientNet-B3 for neoplasia classification, EfficientNet-B5 for ulcer detection/grading), Deep Learning (DL) / Deep Neural Networks (e.g., DNUC for real-time endoscopic and histologic remission scoring), Computer-Aided Detection (CADe) and Computer-Aided Diagnosis (CADx) systems (e.g., EndoBrain, PICaSSO histologic remission index CADe), 3D CNNs, Generative Adversarial Networks (GANs), U-Net, Variational Autoencoder-UNet (VAE-UNet), Random Forests, Multimodal ML Fusion Models, and Natural Language Processing (NLP).

## 1. The "Big Picture" & Motivation
* What is the main problem your research is trying to solve?
Patients with inflammatory bowel disease (IBD; Crohn's disease and ulcerative colitis) face a substantially heightened risk of developing colorectal neoplasia, dysplasia, and colorectal cancer (CRC) driven by chronic mucosal inflammation. Endoscopic surveillance is critical for early detection, but identifying dysplastic lesions is notoriously difficult because lesions are frequently subtle, flat, or obscured by active background inflammation and mucosal irregularities. This results in high rates of missed lesions and significant intra- and interobserver variability among endoscopists.

* What made you choose this specific topic?
While artificial intelligence, machine learning, and deep learning technologies (CADe/CADx) have achieved remarkable success in detecting and diagnosing sporadic colorectal neoplasia in non-IBD screening populations, evidence and dedicated tools specifically focused on the IBD population remain severely limited. Given the distinct morphology of IBD-associated dysplasia and the confounding presence of chronic inflammation, there is an urgent clinical need to evaluate, synthesize, and guide AI applications tailored specifically to IBD surveillance.

* Why does this research matter for the industry right now?
IBD prevalence is steadily increasing worldwide, placing growing demands on surveillance colonoscopy programs. Deploying AI to standardize mucosal activity scoring, automate endoscopic reporting, enhance dysplasia detection sensitivity (particularly for subtle/flat lesions), and guide precise biopsy targeting can prevent CRC progression, enable surgery-sparing endoscopic resections (EMR/ESD), reduce clinical trial costs through automated central reading, and facilitate personalized surveillance regimens.

## 2. The Practical Approach (Methodology)
* Are you doing mostly lab experiments, data analysis, or fieldwork?
This study is a structured literature review and qualitative data synthesis (secondary data analysis) examining peer-reviewed research and review articles published between January 2010 and February 2025, rather than laboratory wet-lab experiments or primary clinical fieldwork.

* What framework or formulas are you using to analyze things?
The review evaluates deep learning, computer vision, and machine learning frameworks—specifically CNN architectures (EfficientNet-B3, EfficientNet-B5, 3D CNNs, U-Net, VAE-UNet), multimodal fusion models, and Random Forests. Performance across reviewed literature is evaluated using agreement and statistical metrics including Quadratic Weighted Kappa (QWK / κ), Intraclass Correlation Coefficient (ICC), Area Under the ROC Curve (AUC / AUROC), Sensitivity, Specificity, Accuracy, Mean Average Precision (MAP), Matthews Correlation Coefficient (MCC), Intersection over Union (IoU), and Decision Curve Analysis (DCA), referenced against clinical endoscopic scoring indices (Mayo Endoscopic Score [MES], UCEIS, SES-CD, CDEIS).

* How do you usually collect your data or samples?
Data was collected through a structured literature search of the PubMed database spanning January 2010 to February 2025 using combinations of keywords ("inflammatory bowel disease," "ulcerative colitis," "Crohn's disease," "dysplasia," "neoplasia," "colorectal cancer," "artificial intelligence," "machine learning," "deep learning," "computer-aided detection," "endoscopy"), followed by manual screening of bibliographies from identified relevant studies.

## 3. Obstacles & Breakthroughs
* What is the biggest challenge you've faced with this project?
The most significant challenge identified is "data set shift" and risk of bias: AI algorithms developed on non-IBD populations fail to generalize to IBD patients due to distinct lesion morphology and background inflammatory changes. Furthermore, IBD-specific datasets suffer from limited sample sizes, lack of external validation cohorts, technical integration barriers with existing endoscopy and electronic health record systems, and high interobserver variability in ground-truth labels.

* Have you hit any dead ends or unexpected results yet?
Early AI algorithms dedicated to IBD dysplasia (e.g., trained on >1,000 images by Vinsard et al.) struggled with flat lesion morphology, demonstrating notably lower detection rates. Additionally, initial CADe models developed for UC biopsy assessment focused on eosinophil counts, which failed to show an association with overall inflammatory disease activity, prompting a required pivot to neutrophil-based evaluation.

* What has been your favorite finding or "eureka" moment?
Training AI algorithms specifically on IBD datasets drastically improves sensitivity and specificity compared to general models. Dedicated deep CNNs (such as EfficientNet-B3 evaluated by Yamamoto et al.) outperformed both expert and non-expert endoscopists (72.5% sensitivity, 82.9% specificity), and real-time CADe systems (Lui et al.) detected 79.1% of missed proximal adenomas and increased overall adenoma detection rates by 23.6%, demonstrating that AI can reliably reveal subtle neoplastic lesions concealed by inflamed mucosa.

## 4. Lit Review & The "Gap"
* What major gap did you find in the previous research?
A critical scarcity of IBD-specific AI training datasets and real-world prospective validation. The vast majority of commercially available CADe/CADx endoscopy tools were trained on sporadic colorectal polyps in non-inflamed mucosa, leading to poor diagnostic specificity, high false-positive rates, and elevated risk of bias when applied to IBD patients.

* Who is the go-to author or foundation paper in your field?
Key foundation authors and landmark studies highlighted include:
- Vinsard et al. (2023): First dedicated AI algorithm trained on over 1,000 images of IBD-associated dysplasia.
- Japanese pioneering case series: First real-time application of CADe (EndoBrain technology) for detecting low- and high-grade dysplasia in IBD surveillance videos.
- Yamamoto et al.: EfficientNet-B3 deep CNN demonstrating superior diagnostic accuracy over human endoscopists for IBD neoplasia classification.
- Takenaka et al. & Stidham et al.: Deep neural networks (DNUC) and computer vision systems for automated scoring of mucosal disease activity and mucosal healing.
- Gottlieb et al. & Byrne et al.: Foundational deep learning models for video-based automated MES and UCEIS scoring in clinical trials.
- Gui et al. / PICaSSO group: Development of neutrophil-based CADe systems (PICaSSO histologic remission index) for automated UC biopsy assessment.

* Did you find any major contradictions between previous studies?
Contradictions emerged when deploying non-IBD-trained CADe models to IBD cohorts: systems that performed exceptionally well on sporadic polyp detection degraded significantly in IBD due to background inflammation. In histopathological assessment, early studies hypothesized eosinophil density as a key marker for AI scoring, whereas subsequent studies proved eosinophil counts did not correlate with clinical disease activity, contrasting with neutrophil-based models that closely match validated indices (Nancy and Robarts indexes).

## 5. Future Impact & Advice
* What do you think is the next step for this research area?
The next essential steps include conducting large-scale prospective multicenter validation trials in diverse IBD cohorts, refining models for flat dysplasia detection, developing automated histology tools (e.g., predicting p53 mutations from H&E slides), integrating multi-omics and EHR data via NLP and ML to predict biologic treatment response (e.g., infliximab, vedolizumab, ustekinumab) and disease flare, and establishing standardized regulatory and ethical frameworks.

* Where do you see your findings being applied in real life?
Direct real-time clinical deployment in endoscopy suites providing bounding-box overlays for dysplasia detection during surveillance colonoscopies, automated software for endoscopy report generation and clinical trial central reading, digital pathology workflows for rapid biopsy assessment, and AI-driven clinical decision support systems integrated into EHRs to guide personalized medical therapies and surveillance intervals.

* Any tips for someone trying to read papers in this domain?
Always check whether an AI model was trained and validated on IBD-specific cohorts versus general non-IBD screening datasets; examine whether evaluations used real-time full-length endoscopy videos or curated static images; assess performance across different lesion morphologies (especially flat vs. elevated/polypoid); and scrutinize standard statistical agreement metrics (e.g., Quadratic Weighted Kappa, Intraclass Correlation Coefficient, and AUC) to judge clinical generalizability.

## [Revolutionizing Medical Imaging: The Transformative Role of Artificial Intelligence in Diagnostics and Treatment]
**Core ML Technology:** The paper highlights various ML and deep learning architectures including U-Net, UNeXt, YOLO (v8 and v10), Random Forest, and a hybrid CNN–ViT (Convolutional Neural Network – Vision Transformer) framework for multi-class classification in medical imaging.

## 1. The "Big Picture" & Motivation
* What is the main problem your research is trying to solve?
The research aims to explore the transformative role of Artificial Intelligence (AI) in medical imaging, specifically addressing the need to enhance diagnostic precision, clinical efficiency, and treatment personalization.
* What made you choose this specific topic?
The rapid integration of AI and machine learning into clinical workflows and research methodologies, creating a paradigm shift in how medical images are interpreted and utilized for diagnostics.
* Why does this research matter for the industry right now?
It matters because AI tools are becoming indispensable for supporting clinicians, reducing human error, enabling early detection of pathologies (e.g., cancer, cardiac conditions), and streamlining clinical workflows, thereby improving patient outcomes.

## 2. The Practical Approach (Methodology)
* Are you doing mostly lab experiments, data analysis, or fieldwork?
The Special Issue comprises a collection of research articles (15) and reviews (5) based on data analysis of medical imaging datasets (e.g., CT, MRI, X-rays, echocardiography) to evaluate AI models.
* What framework or formulas are you using to analyze things?
The studies employ various frameworks and models, including deep learning architectures (U-Net, UNeXt, CNN–ViT), object detection models (YOLOv8, YOLOv10), and machine learning classifiers (Random Forest).
* How do you usually collect your data or samples?
Data collection involves standard medical imaging modalities such as Computed Tomography (CT), Magnetic Resonance Imaging (MRI), transesophageal echocardiography (TEE), and chest X-rays.

## 3. Obstacles & Breakthroughs
* What is the biggest challenge you've faced with this project?
While not explicitly personal, the paper notes general challenges in AI integration, such as data governance (privacy/security), the need for standardized imaging protocols for reproducibility, and ensuring transparency and accountability.
* Have you hit any dead ends or unexpected results yet?
The paper does not detail specific failed experiments, but emphasizes the need for ongoing refinement of algorithms to improve reliability and explainability.
* What has been your favorite finding or "eureka" moment?
The highlight is the demonstration of AI’s efficacy across diverse specialties—from liver and skin cancer detection to aortic dissection diagnosis and pediatric dental care—showcasing its versatility.

## 4. Lit Review & The "Gap"
* What major gap did you find in the previous research?
The paper builds upon the first edition of the Special Issue, implying a need for continued exploration of the "evolving landscape" of AI applications as technology progresses.
* Who is the go-to author or foundation paper in your field?
The paper references multiple specific contributions (e.g., Brunese et al., Inmutto et al., etc.) rather than a single foundation paper, highlighting the collaborative international nature of the field.
* Did you find any major contradictions between previous studies?
No major contradictions are reported; the studies collectively underscore advancements and shared goals in enhancing diagnostic accuracy and clinical workflows.

## 5. Future Impact & Advice
* What do you think is the next step for this research area?
Future steps include the refinement of AI algorithms for better reliability and explainability, expansion into predictive analytics for early disease progression, and the integration of multi-modal data.
* Where do you see your findings being applied in real life?
The findings are currently applied in clinical environments to support radiologists, oncologists, and other specialists in tailoring patient-specific interventions and optimizing radiotherapy and surgical planning.
* Any tips for someone trying to read papers in this domain?
While not explicitly stated, the context suggests focusing on how specific AI architectures (CNNs, Transformers) are mapped to clinical needs (segmentation, classification, prediction).

## Deep learning model for gastrointestinal polyp segmentation (DOI: 10.7717/peerj-cs.2924)
**Core ML Technology:** Hybrid Encoder-Decoder Architecture integrating a pre-trained ConvNeXt feature extractor, Cross-Attention skip connections, and Residual Transformer Blocks with multi-head self-attention.

## 1. The "Big Picture" & Motivation
* What is the main problem your research is trying to solve?
Colorectal cancer is one of the leading causes of cancer-related mortality globally, developing from benign polyps that can progress into malignant neoplasms. While colonoscopy is the gold-standard screening method, polyp detection and accurate boundary segmentation remain difficult due to high visual heterogeneity in polyp size, shape, and texture, presence of blind spots, and reader interpretation variability. Clinical studies indicate a polyp miss rate of 22% to 28% (up to 26% for small adenomas). The research aims to solve automated, highly accurate gastrointestinal polyp segmentation and boundary delineation from colonoscopy images to minimize missed detections and support clinical interventions.

* What made you choose this specific topic?
Precise polyp segmentation directly influences clinical colorectal cancer management by enabling accurate morphological parameter evaluation, which is vital for planning endoscopic resection techniques (such as "resect and discard" paradigms) and longitudinal surveillance. Although deep learning models exist, conventional CNNs suffer from spatial feature degradation in deep layers, and pure vision transformers often struggle with boundary localization on limited medical datasets. Developing a hybrid framework combining modernized convolutions with attention mechanisms bridges the gap between research developments and clinical utility.

* Why does this research matter for the industry right now?
Integrating robust AI-driven decision-support systems into endoscopic workflows provides standardized, clinician-independent assistance in real-time. By simultaneously achieving high precision (0.9023) and recall (0.8796), this technology reduces false negatives (preventing missed precancerous lesions) and false positives (avoiding unnecessary resections), shortens examination times, and enhances patient prognosis in colorectal cancer screening.

## 2. The Practical Approach (Methodology)
* Are you doing mostly lab experiments, data analysis, or fieldwork?
The study focuses on computational data analysis, deep learning modeling, and experimental benchmarking. The network was implemented in PyTorch and trained on a local workstation equipped with a single NVIDIA RTX 3060 GPU (12 GB VRAM), CUDA 14.2, and Windows 11 using offline curated endoscopic image datasets.

* What framework or formulas are you using to analyze things?
The methodology uses PyTorch with an AdamW optimizer (learning rate = 0.0001, weight decay = 1e-4, batch size = 16, 50 epochs).
- **Segmentation Mapping:** Pixel-wise classification $L: \Omega \to \{0, 1, \dots, K-1\}$ and model prediction function $\hat{L} = F(I; \theta)$.
- **Loss Functions:** Combined Cross-Entropy and Dice Loss:
$$\mathcal{L}_{Dice} = 1 - \frac{2 \sum_i p_i g_i}{\sum_i p_i^2 + \sum_i g_i^2}$$
- **Attention Formulas:** Scaled Dot-Product Cross-Attention:
$$\text{Attention scores} = \frac{Q K^T}{\sqrt{d_k}}$$
$$\text{Attention weights} = \text{Softmax}\left(\frac{Q K^T}{\sqrt{d_k}}\right)$$
$$\text{Output} = \text{Attention weights} \times V$$
- **Evaluation Metrics:**
$$\text{Dice}(A,B) = \frac{2 \times |A \cap B|}{|A| + |B|} = \frac{2 \times TP}{2 \times TP + FP + FN}$$
$$\text{mIoU}(A,B) = \frac{|A \cap B|}{|A \cup B|} = \frac{TP}{TP + FP + FN}$$
$$\text{Recall} = \frac{TP}{TP + FN}, \quad \text{Precision} = \frac{TP}{TP + FP}$$

* How do you usually collect your data or samples?
The experiments utilize the publicly available benchmark Kvasir-SEG dataset, comprising 1,000 high-resolution colonoscopy polyp images with pixel-level ground truth masks verified by experienced gastroenterologists. The dataset is expanded via offline data augmentations (random cropping, flipping, rotation, cutoff, brightness shifts, scaling, random erasing, contrast adjustments, and Gaussian noise) resized to $320 \times 320$ pixels, and partitioned into an 80% training, 10% validation, and 10% testing split.

## 3. Obstacles & Breakthroughs
* What is the biggest challenge you've faced with this project?
The primary challenge is segmenting small, flat, or sessile polyps that have low contrast against surrounding mucosal folds, as well as handling image noise, lighting variations, and occlusion in endoscopic video frames without incurring high computational latency.

* Have you hit any dead ends or unexpected results yet?
Ablation experiments revealed that pure Vision Transformers (ViT) underperform compared to convolutional backbones due to their lack of intrinsic spatial inductive bias on smaller medical datasets. Furthermore, visualization analysis confirmed that while well-demarcated polyps segment accurately, subtle, flat, and partially occluded lesions still show occasional boundary under-segmentation.

* What has been your favorite finding or "eureka" moment?
The synergy of using a pre-trained ConvNeXt encoder paired with scaled dot-product cross-attention shortcut connections and Residual Transformer Blocks in the decoder:
1. ConvNeXt outperformed classical CNN backbones (VGG16, ResNet50) and pure transformers (ViT), achieving a validation mIoU of 0.8120 and DSC of 0.8802.
2. Cross-attention effectively restored fine spatial details lost during deep feature downsampling, allowing the complete architecture to surpass state-of-the-art benchmarks including SegFormer and Swin Transformer with a test DSC of 0.8715 (0.8659) and mIoU of 0.8021.

## 4. Lit Review & The "Gap"
* What major gap did you find in the previous research?
Traditional methods (e.g., fuzzy clustering, Otsu thresholding) fail when encountering high anatomical variability. Standard CNN architectures (e.g., U-Net, U-Net++, ResUNet++) suffer from feature loss and limited global context in deep layers. Conversely, transformer architectures often require massive training datasets and lack fine local spatial boundary delineation. A gap existed for a computationally balanced model that combines strong local spatial feature retention with long-range contextual dependencies.

* Who is the go-to author or foundation paper in your field?
- **Ronneberger et al. (2015):** U-Net architecture foundational to biomedical image segmentation.
- **Jha et al. (2019a, 2019b, 2021):** Authors of the Kvasir-SEG benchmark dataset, ResUNet++, and ColonSegNet.
- **Liu et al. (2022):** ConvNeXt architecture ("A ConvNet for the 2020s").
- **Vaswani et al. (2017):** Foundational Transformer self-attention mechanism.
- **Zhou et al. (2018, 2023):** U-Net++ and hybrid spatial-channel attention networks for polyp segmentation.

* Did you find any major contradictions between previous studies?
While recent literature often asserts that pure transformer models uniformly supersede convolutional networks across vision benchmarks, empirical evaluations in medical image segmentation show that pure ViTs struggle on standard-sized medical datasets (like Kvasir-SEG). Modernized convolutional networks (ConvNeXt) retain essential inductive biases and outperform pure transformers unless transformer blocks are selectively hybridized with convolutional stages and cross-attention.

## 5. Future Impact & Advice
* What do you think is the next step for this research area?
Future steps include expanding training to multi-center, cross-device clinical datasets with domain harmonization, developing contrast-aware enhancement modules specifically for flat/occluded lesions, and applying model quantization, pruning, or lightweight hybrid designs to facilitate real-time inference on edge devices in clinical endoscopy suites.

* Where do you see your findings being applied in real life?
Direct integration into real-time computer-aided detection/diagnosis (CADe/CADx) endoscopy systems and smart colonoscopy video processors to assist clinicians in live lesion detection, margin delineation for polypectomy, and in vivo polyp characterization.

* Any tips for someone trying to read papers in this domain?
Focus on how architectures balance local spatial inductive bias with global attention mechanisms; look closely at clinically relevant evaluation metrics (DSC, mIoU, Recall, Precision) rather than overall accuracy alone; and carefully examine ablation studies and failure cases on low-contrast or flat lesions to understand real-world clinical feasibility.


---

## Artificial Intelligence in Endoscopic and Ultrasound Imaging for Inflammatory Bowel Disease (DOI: 10.3390/jcm14124291)
**Core ML Technology:** Deep Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs, recurrent CNNs, Single Shot MultiBox Detectors [SSD], pre-trained CNN backbones), Computer-Aided Detection/Diagnosis systems (CAD/CADe/CADx such as EndoBRAIN-EYE, CADe Discovery™, ENDOANGEL, AXARO®), pixel/color propensity algorithms (e.g., Red Density algorithm using RGB color extraction and redness maps), radiomics-based machine learning texture analysis, Recurrent Neural Networks (RNNs) for temporal image sequences, contrast-enhanced ultrasound (CEUS) time-intensity curve quantification, 2D shear-wave elastography (SWE) modeling, and multimodal "endo-omics" frameworks.

## 1. The "Big Picture" & Motivation
* What is the main problem your research is trying to solve?
Inflammatory bowel diseases (IBD), including Crohn’s disease (CD) and ulcerative colitis (UC), require continuous and accurate evaluation for diagnosis, disease activity monitoring, and therapeutic adjustments. However, conventional diagnostic imaging modalities—conventional endoscopy, video capsule endoscopy (VCE), and intestinal ultrasound (IUS)—suffer from significant inter- and intra-observer variability (agreement rates ranging from 0.58 to 0.80), subjective disease scoring, prolonged reading times (VCE often takes over an hour per patient, inducing reader fatigue), operator dependence (especially in ultrasound), and uneven access to expert-level interpretation. This research examines how artificial intelligence (AI) can overcome these limitations by automating lesion detection, standardizing severity scoring (e.g., Mayo Endoscopic Score [MES], UCEIS, SES-CD, IBUS-SAS), reducing interpretation times, providing objective quality control metrics, and differentiating inflammatory from fibrotic strictures.

* What made you choose this specific topic?
While several recent reviews have addressed AI in IBD imaging, previous literature has remained narrowly focused on isolated modalities (such as colonoscopy alone or ultrasound alone) and lacked a cohesive clinical workflow perspective. The authors chose this topic to provide a comprehensive, multi-modal synthesis integrating conventional endoscopy, video capsule endoscopy, and intestinal ultrasound, while emphasizing procedural quality metrics (e.g., Effective Withdrawal Time, bowel preparation scores), real-world clinical implementation challenges (data heterogeneity, model explainability, clinician acceptance, and regulatory hurdles), external multicenter validation rigor, and emerging translational concepts such as endo-omics and AI-driven fibrosis assessment.

* Why does this research matter for the industry right now?
IBD management and colorectal cancer surveillance represent a substantial clinical and economic burden on healthcare systems. AI systems are currently transitioning from experimental prototypes into commercial platforms and regulatory pathways in gastroenterology (e.g., PillCam™, CADe Discovery™, ENDOANGEL, EndoBRAIN-EYE). Demonstrating that AI can increase adenoma detection rates (ADR by 20%), decrease adenoma miss rates (by 55%), reduce VCE reading times by over 70%, and accurately infer histological remission without requiring physical mucosal biopsies offers immediate value—streamlining clinical workflows, mitigating diagnostic delays, lowering procedural costs, reducing clinician burnout, and standardizing endpoints in multi-center clinical trials.

## 2. The Practical Approach (Methodology)
* Are you doing mostly lab experiments, data analysis, or fieldwork?
This study is a structured comprehensive systematic review and data synthesis of peer-reviewed clinical cohorts, multi-center trials, randomized controlled trials (RCTs), and technical validation reports published between January 2018 and March 2025 across PubMed, Scopus, and Web of Science databases.

* What framework or formulas are you using to analyze things?
- **Clinical & Imaging Scoring Indices:** Mayo Endoscopic Score (MES), Ulcerative Colitis Endoscopic Index of Severity (UCEIS), Simple Endoscopic Score for CD (SES-CD), International Bowel Ultrasound Segmental Activity Score (IBUS-SAS), Crohn’s Disease Activity Index (CDAI), Harvey–Bradshaw Index (HBI), and modified Limberg scores.
- **Methodological & AI Quality Appraisal Frameworks:** QUADAS-2 (Quality Assessment of Diagnostic Accuracy Studies) for diagnostic accuracy, CLAIM (Checklist for Artificial Intelligence in Medical Imaging) checklist for AI reporting transparency, and SPIRIT-AI / CONSORT-AI guidelines.
- **Statistical & Evaluation Metrics:** Area Under the Receiver Operating Characteristic Curve (AUROC / AUC), sensitivity, specificity, accuracy, Intraclass Correlation Coefficient (ICC), Cohen’s kappa (κ), Pearson/Spearman correlation coefficients (r), Adenoma Detection Rate (ADR), Adenoma Miss Rate (AMR), Effective Withdrawal Time (EWT), and Fold Examination Quality (FEQ).
- **Algorithmic Frameworks:** Deep CNN architectures, recurrent CNNs, Single Shot MultiBox Detectors (SSD), Radiomics-based high-throughput texture analysis, and the Red Density RGB color-propensity algorithm for vascular redness extraction.

* How do you usually collect your data or samples?
- **In Reviewed Primary Studies:** Data were collected across clinical cohorts via high-definition white light colonoscopy image/video repositories (e.g., >26,000 to >40,000 images, 6,800 biopsy correlations, and 795 full-length videos with ~20 million frames), wireless capsule endoscopy video feeds (PillCam™, AXARO®), transabdominal intestinal ultrasound (IUS) B-mode cine loops, contrast-enhanced ultrasound (CEUS) time–intensity curves, and 2D shear-wave elastography (SWE) stiffness acquisitions.
- **In This Review Manuscript:** Data were systematically gathered through structured electronic database queries (PubMed, Scopus, Web of Science) from Jan 2018 to Mar 2025 using predefined combinations of keywords ("artificial intelligence," "machine learning," "deep learning," "endoscopy," "ultrasound," "capsule endoscopy," "Crohn’s disease," "ulcerative colitis"), followed by rigorous CLAIM/QUADAS-2 quality screening, excluding non-human data and isolated case reports.

## 3. Obstacles & Breakthroughs
* What is the biggest challenge you've faced with this project?
The primary challenges identified are: (1) Data quality, fragmentation, and lack of standardized acquisition protocols across different endoscope and ultrasound manufacturers, (2) The labor-intensive nature and interobserver variability of manual ground-truth image annotations, (3) The "black box" nature of deep neural networks, which hampers interpretability and clinician trust during critical treatment-decision points, and (4) Operator dependence in intestinal ultrasound acquisition, which introduces cross-center variability that challenges model generalizability.

* Have you hit any dead ends or unexpected results yet?
- Conventional deep CNN architectures alone underperformed in analyzing transabdominal intestinal ultrasound (IUS) features and distinguishing complex tissue changes, achieving an AUC of only 0.75 compared to 0.98 achieved by radiomics-based texture analysis.
- Standard IUS currently lacks sufficient diagnostic precision across published studies to reliably differentiate inflammatory from fibrotic strictures due to inconsistent definitions and imaging protocols across centers.
- Real-world, day-to-day clinical practice data for many AI computer-aided detection (CAD) tools remains scarce, and in certain virtual chromoendoscopy settings, CAD systems demonstrated diagnostic performance slightly lower than expert endoscopists (though still outperforming non-experts).

* What has been your favorite finding or "eureka" moment?
- Deep learning CNNs can infer *histological* remission from endoscopic mucosal images alone with up to 92.9% accuracy, pointing toward a future where expensive, invasive physical biopsies could be significantly minimized or avoided.
- AI systems applied to video capsule endoscopy can reduce reading times by over 70% (e.g., from 12.2 to 3.1 minutes for experts) or condense datasets down to 2.1% of original frames without missing clinically relevant mucosal lesions.
- Real-time procedural AI assistants (such as ENDOANGEL and Effective Withdrawal Time metrics) actively guide colonoscopists in real time, doubling adenoma detection rates (from 8% to 16%) and ensuring standardized mucosal visualization quality.

## 4. Lit Review & The "Gap"
* What major gap did you find in the previous research?
Previous reviews operated in silos—focusing solely on conventional colonoscopy or intestinal ultrasound in isolation—without providing a unified, multi-modal perspective across endoscopy, video capsule endoscopy, and ultrasound. Furthermore, prior research emphasized static benchmark algorithm accuracy while neglecting procedural quality metrics (withdrawal speed, bowel prep scoring, fold examination quality), real-world implementation challenges (EHR integration, model interpretability, regulatory hurdles), and systematic appraisal of prospective, multicenter external validation.

* Who is the go-to author or foundation paper in your field?
Key foundation authors and studies highlighted include:
- **Raf Bisschops and colleagues (2020):** Developed the foundational Red Density pixel-propensity algorithm for objective, real-time endoscopic disease activity scoring in UC.
- **Aoki et al. (2019, 2020):** Pioneered Single Shot MultiBox Detector CNN models for automated lesion detection and reading-time reduction in video capsule endoscopy.
- **Gottlieb et al. (2021):** Developed recurrent CNN models trained on 795 full-length colonoscopy videos (~20 million frames) for automated Mayo and UCEIS score evaluation.
- **Maeda et al. / EndoBRAIN Group (2019, 2021):** Validated endocytoscopy and CAD systems (EndoBRAIN-EYE) for detecting histologic inflammation and dysplasia in longstanding UC.
- **Novak et al. / International Bowel Ultrasound Group (2021):** Established the International Bowel Ultrasound Segmental Activity Score (IBUS-SAS) standardizing ultrasound assessment in CD.
- **Stidham et al. (2019):** Demonstrated expert-level CNN grading of UC severity (AUROC 0.97).
- **Iacucci et al. (2023):** Introduced the paradigm of "endo-omics" fusing AI computer vision with molecular, histological, and clinical data.

* Did you find any major contradictions between previous studies?
- **CNNs vs. Radiomics in Ultrasound:** While standard CNNs represent the gold standard in endoscopic image analysis, they proved inferior to radiomics-based texture analysis when applied to intestinal ultrasound classification (AUC 0.75 for CNN vs. 0.98 for radiomics), demonstrating that different modalities require fundamentally distinct AI architectures.
- **Stricture Characterization Discrepancies:** While smaller prospective single-center studies reported high correlation between ultrasound features (e.g., color Doppler Limberg scores and hyperechogenic spiculates) and histopathological stricture types, large systematic reviews (e.g., Lu et al., 56 studies) revealed significant cross-study contradictions, highlighting that standard IUS alone cannot yet reliably differentiate inflammatory from fibrotic strictures in clinical practice.
- **Research Scope Divergence:** Earlier AI research primarily targeted therapeutic response prediction, whereas subsequent literature shifted predominantly toward diagnostic lesion detection and image classification, leaving a gap in unified prognostic models.

## 5. Future Impact & Advice
* What do you think is the next step for this research area?
The next major frontier is the development of multimodal "endo-omics" platforms that integrate computer vision imaging with multi-omics data streams (genomics, transcriptomics, gut microbiome, and serum biomarkers) alongside clinical parameters. Additionally, future efforts must focus on prospective, randomized multicenter clinical trials designed under SPIRIT-AI/CONSORT-AI guidelines, refining point-of-care ultrasound AI algorithms (shear-wave elastography, automated bowel wall thickness, Doppler vascularity) connected to telemedicine, and leveraging AI in clinical trials to automate patient screening and generate synthetic control arms.

* Where do you see your findings being applied in real life?
- **Real-Time Clinical Endoscopy Suites:** Point-of-care computer-aided detection (CADe) and quality assurance systems (e.g., ENDOANGEL) providing live feedback on withdrawal speed, bowel preparation, and dysplasia recognition.
- **High-Throughput VCE Triage:** Automated first-pass filtering algorithms in gastrointestinal reading centers, reducing video review times from over an hour to under five minutes per patient.
- **Bedside Intestinal Ultrasound Clinics:** Radiation-free, non-invasive point-of-care IUS in adult and pediatric clinics to dynamically evaluate transmural healing and guide treat-to-target therapy adjustments.
- **Standardized Multi-Center Clinical Trials:** Automated, centralized objective scoring of endoscopic and ultrasound endpoints, reducing inter-observer bias and clinical trial operational costs.

* Any tips for someone trying to read papers in this domain?
- Critically check whether the AI model was validated on independent, external multicenter datasets or prospective cohorts versus merely internal cross-validation on retrospective, single-center data.
- Distinguish between benchmark accuracy on curated static images (which often inflates performance) and real-time performance on full-length video streams or live bedside examinations (evaluating frame rate, latency, and false-positive burden).
- Verify whether studies report clinical and histological correlation endpoints (e.g., mucosal healing, histologic remission, transmural healing) and adhere to standardized reporting guidelines such as CLAIM, QUADAS-2, SPIRIT-AI, and CONSORT-AI.

## Revolutionizing gastroenterology and hepatology with artificial intelligence: From precision diagnosis to equitable healthcare through interdisciplinary practice

## Abstract

Artificial intelligence (AI) is driving a paradigm shift in gastroenterology and hepatology by delivering cutting-edge tools for disease screening, diagnosis, treatment, and prognostic management. Through deep learning, radiomics, and multimodal data integration, AI has achieved diagnostic parity with expert clinicians in endoscopic image analysis (e.g., early gastric cancer detection, colorectal polyp identification) and non-invasive assessment of liver pathologies (e.g., fibrosis staging, fatty liver typing) while demonstrating utility in personalized care scenarios such as predicting hepatocellular carcinoma recurrence and optimizing inflammatory bowel disease treatment responses. Despite these advancements challenges persist including limited model generalization due to fragmented datasets, algorithmic limitations in rare conditions (e.g., pediatric liver diseases) caused by insufficient training data, and unresolved ethical issues related to bias, accountability, and patient privacy. Mitigation strategies involve constructing standardized multicenter databases, validating AI tools through prospective trials, leveraging federated learning to address data scarcity, and developing interpretable systems (e.g., attention heatmap visualization) to enhance clinical trust. Integrating generative AI, digital twin technologies, and establishing unified ethical/regulatory frameworks will accelerate AI adoption in primary care and foster equitable healthcare access while interdisciplinary collaboration and evidence-based implementation remain critical for realizing AI’s potential to redefine precision care for digestive disorders, improve global health outcomes, and reshape healthcare equity.

## Core Tip

This review highlights artificial intelligence (AI)-driven innovations in gastroenterology and hepatology, demonstrating breakthroughs in endoscopic/image analysis, multi-omics integration, and precision therapy. AI achieves diagnostic parity with experts in detecting early cancers and fibrosis, while addressing challenges like data fragmentation and bias through standardized databases, federated learning, and explainable systems. The study emphasizes interdisciplinary collaboration and ethical frameworks to advance equitable healthcare access and redefine digestive disease management.

## INTRODUCTION

The development of artificial intelligence (AI) applications in medicine dates back to the mid-20th century, and the combination of its technological evolution and medical needs has driven several key breakthroughs. Early explorations were represented by expert systems, such as the MYCIN system developed in 1975, which first realized rule-based diagnosis of infectious diseases. This phase of research laid the foundation for subsequent AI logical reasoning in medicine, but the scope of application was limited due to the scale of data and computational power at that time. Machine learning technology emerged from the 1990s to the beginning of the 21st century.

The current medical AI technology system covers machine learning, deep learning and natural language processing technologies.

Global cancer statistics for 2022 show that cancers of the digestive tract are one of the major threats to human life and health, with esophageal, gastric, liver, and colorectal cancers having the seventh, fifth, third, and second highest mortality rates in the world, respectively.

## BREAKTHROUGHS IN AI FOR GASTROINTESTINAL DISEASE DIAGNOSIS AND TREATMENT

Recent advancements in AI have catalyzed transformative applications across gastrointestinal disease diagnostics and therapeutics, with machine learning and deep learning emerging as distinct yet complementary paradigms.

## AI applications for inflammatory gastrointestinal diseases

AI demonstrates multidimensional value in the management of UC and Crohn’s disease. In the field of UC, deep learning-based histologic scoring systems (e.g., PICaSSO histologic remission index) are highly correlated with endoscopic scores (Mayo, PICaSSO) and clinical outcomes.

## Recent advances in AI-enabled upper gastrointestinal tumor diagnosis and treatment

Deep learning-based endoscopic image analysis system significantly improves the efficiency and detection rate of upper gastrointestinal tumors.

## AI enabled colorectal tumor diagnosis and treatment: Recent advances and clinical translation

AI becomes a real-time aid in colonoscopy; traditional colonoscopy suffers from the limitation of a high rate of adenoma leakage (15%-30%), which has been significantly improved by deep learning-based computer-aided detection (CADe) systems through real-time polyp identification.

## AI-driven innovations in hepatic oncology: From multiscale diagnostics to precision therapeutics

The degree of liver fibrosis is the most sensitive clinical warning sign for metabolic dysfunction-associated steatotic liver disease (MASLD)-associated hepatocellular carcinoma.

## Deep integration of gut microbes and machine learning: Decoding cross-disease signatures to drive precision therapies

In the study of gut microbiology in inflammatory bowel disease, machine learning techniques have systematically deconstructed the gut-type-specific pathogenicity network of UC.

## VMDU-net: a dual encoder multi-scale fusion network for polyp segmentation with Vision Mamba and Cross-Shape Transformer integration (DOI: 10.3389/frai.2025.1557508)
**Core ML Technology:** VMDU-Net is a dual-encoder multi-scale feature fusion architecture that combines a Vision Mamba Encoder (bidirectional 2D State Space Model) and a custom Cross-Shape Transformer (CSA with grouped horizontal and vertical strip self-attention, $s_w=3$) alongside auxiliary Depthwise Separable Convolutions (DWConv) for local inductive bias and a Mamba-Transformer-Merge (MTM) module for spatial-channel feature fusion, trained from scratch using Adam optimizer and combined Binary Cross-Entropy and Dice loss on 4× Quadro RTX 8000 GPUs.

## 1. The "Big Picture" & Motivation
* What is the main problem your research is trying to solve?
The research aims to solve the challenge of accurate automated colorectal polyp segmentation in colonoscopy images. Polyps exhibit extensive morphological diversity, varying sizes, subtle textures, and blurred boundaries lacking clear contrast with surrounding mucosal tissue. Furthermore, existing deep learning algorithms face a persistent dilemma: CNNs have limited local receptive fields that miss global contextual dependencies, standard Vision Transformers incur quadratic computational complexity and suffer from slow, unstable training convergence, and pure Mamba (SSM) models underperform in capturing deep, long-context semantic interactions.

* What made you choose this specific topic?
Colorectal cancer (CRC) is the third most common malignant tumor globally, predominantly developing from precancerous colorectal polyps. Early detection and endoscopic resection during routine screening colonoscopy can reduce CRC incidence by up to 30%. Because manual examination is prone to missed diagnoses due to operator fatigue and subtle polyp appearances, developing a robust automated segmentation algorithm is of critical clinical importance for early CRC prevention.

* Why does this research matter for the industry right now?
In clinical endoscopy and computer-aided diagnosis (CADe/CADx) systems, real-time automated segmentation provides objective, high-precision visual assistance to endoscopists. VMDU-Net delivers state-of-the-art segmentation accuracy (Dice scores up to 0.964 on CVC-ClinicDB) while maintaining efficient inference latency (85.8 ms per image), bridging the gap between high boundary precision and computational practicality in clinical screening workflows.

## 2. The Practical Approach (Methodology)
* Are you doing mostly lab experiments, data analysis, or fieldwork?
The work consists of computational machine learning experiments, empirical benchmarking, ablation studies, and retrospective data analysis conducted on five publicly available clinical colonoscopy benchmark datasets.

* What framework or formulas are you using to analyze things?
The model is implemented in PyTorch 1.10 within the MMsegmentation framework. Key formulas and structural components include:
- **Cross-Shape Self-Attention (CSA):** Features are linearly projected into $K$ heads partitioned into two groups ($K/2$ horizontal strips and $K/2$ vertical strips of width $s_w$). Attention is computed as:
  $$\text{Att}_k(X) = \text{Softmax}\left(\frac{Q_k K_k^T}{\sqrt{d_k}}\right)V_k$$
  with outputs projected via $W^O$: $\text{CSA}(X) = \text{Concat}(\text{HAtt}_1, \dots, \text{VAtt}_K) W^O$.
- **Vision Mamba Encoder (VME):** Employs bidirectional 2D sequence scanning with 1D convolutions and linear projections to compute state space parameters $B, C, \Delta$, applying a Softplus transform to $D$ for continuous temporal scaling.
- **Mamba-Transformer-Merge (MTM):** Fuses CST and VME feature maps via coordinate-inspired spatial pooling across height ($Z_h \in \mathbb{R}^{W \times C}$) and width ($Z_w \in \mathbb{R}^{H \times C}$), followed by 4 fully connected channel reduction branches ($f_1, f_2, f_3, f_4$) and outer matrix multiplication ($O = O_h \times O_w$).
- **Loss Function:** Joint Binary Cross-Entropy and Dice loss:
  $$L_{\text{Total}} = \alpha L_{\text{BCE}} + \beta L_{\text{Dice}} \quad (\text{with } \alpha = \beta = 1)$$
- **Evaluation Metrics:** Mean Intersection over Union (mIoU), Dice coefficient, Precision, and Recall.

* How do you usually collect your data or samples?
Data was curated from five public colonoscopy datasets:
1. **Kvasir-SEG** (1,000 annotated images, resolutions from $332 \times 487$ to $1920 \times 1072$).
2. **CVC-ClinicDB** (612 frames from 31 colonoscopy sequences, $384 \times 288$).
3. **EndoScene** (912 annotated images).
4. **CVC-ColonDB** (380 standardized images from 15 sequences, $574 \times 500$).
5. **ETIS** (192 high-resolution images from 29 sequences, $1225 \times 996$).
The training set merged Kvasir-SEG and CVC-ClinicDB (trained from scratch for 5,000 iterations, batch size 8, Adam optimizer, initial LR $1 \times 10^{-4}$ with polynomial decay power 0.9, input size $384 \times 384$). In-distribution evaluation was performed on Kvasir-SEG and CVC-ClinicDB test sets, while EndoScene, CVC-ColonDB, and ETIS served as out-of-distribution zero-shot test sets.

## 3. Obstacles & Breakthroughs
* What is the biggest challenge you've faced with this project?
The primary challenge was balancing the extraction of long-range global contextual dependencies (necessary for identifying camouflaged or large polyps) with the preservation of localized boundary details and fast, stable training convergence without relying on pre-trained weights.

* Have you hit any dead ends or unexpected results yet?
Standard patch-based Vision Transformers struggled with slow convergence and lack of inductive bias on medical data, while pure Mamba models lacked the depth required for long-context boundary refinement. Additionally, tuning the strip width $s_w$ in the CSA module demonstrated that a small width ($s_w=1$) failed to capture sufficient global context, whereas larger widths ($s_w=5, 7$) slowed convergence; $s_w=3$ was established as optimal. Furthermore, the dual-encoder architecture introduces higher parameter overhead compared to pure Mamba networks.

* What has been your favorite finding or "eureka" moment?
The breakthrough was the synergistic interaction between Mamba, Cross-Shape Transformer, and Depthwise Separable Convolution: Vision Mamba provides rapid global dependency modeling in early training iterations, DWConv supplies local spatial inductive priors that dramatically accelerate convergence, and CST extracts deep horizontal and vertical contextual features in later iterations. This combination allowed VMDU-Net to achieve superior performance across all benchmarks, including a Dice score of 0.715 on the difficult ETIS dataset (where U-Net scored only 0.401 and ResUnet scored 0.152).

## 4. Lit Review & The "Gap"
* What major gap did you find in the previous research?
Prior research relied either on pure CNNs (limited receptive field, poor global contextual modeling), pure Vision Transformers (high computational burden, slow convergence, lack of inductive bias), or pure State Space Models (suboptimal long-context semantic modeling compared to attention). No prior framework integrated bidirectional State Space Models with cross-shaped strip attention and depthwise convolutional bias into a multi-scale dual-encoder architecture for medical segmentation.

* Who is the go-to author or foundation paper in your field?
- **U-Net & Medical CNNs:** Ronneberger et al. (2015) (*U-Net*), Zhou et al. (2018) (*U-Net++*), Diakogiannis et al. (2020) (*ResUNet*), Fan et al. (2020) (*PraNet*), Jha et al. (2019) (*Kvasir-SEG / ResUNet++*).
- **Vision Transformers:** Vaswani et al. (2017) (*Transformer*), Dosovitskiy et al. (2020) (*ViT*), Liu et al. (2021) (*Swin Transformer*), Chen et al. (2021) (*TransUNet*), Dong et al. (2021) (*Polyp-PVT*).
- **State Space Models & Vision Mamba:** Gu and Dao (2023) (*Mamba*), Zhu et al. (2024) (*Vision Mamba / Vim*), Ma et al. (2024) (*U-Mamba*), Ruan et al. (2024) (*VM-UNet*).

* Did you find any major contradictions between previous studies?
While recent Mamba literature asserted that SSMs could fully replace Transformers in vision tasks with lower computational complexity, empirical evidence from Waleffe et al. (2024) and this study demonstrated that Transformers still hold an advantage in modeling ultra-long context dependencies and complex boundaries. VMDU-Net reconciles this contradiction by leveraging both architectures in parallel rather than replacing one with the other.

## 5. Future Impact & Advice
* What do you think is the next step for this research area?
Future steps include structural optimization and model compression (e.g., pruning, knowledge distillation) to reduce parameter counts and inference latency for resource-constrained clinical edge devices, expanding evaluation to diverse multi-center clinical cohorts to mitigate annotation subjectivity and class imbalance, and extending the architecture to 3D/volumetric and video-based medical segmentation.

* Where do you see your findings being applied in real life?
The architecture can be integrated into real-time Computer-Aided Detection (CADe) and Diagnosis (CADx) systems within clinical endoscopy suites, providing live polyp detection, automated margin contouring during polypectomies, and post-procedure quality assurance audits.

* Any tips for someone trying to read papers in this domain?
Understand the distinct inductive biases and mathematical mechanisms of CNNs (local sliding kernels), Transformers (pairwise self-attention), and State Space Models (continuous linear recurrent state-space representations). When evaluating medical segmentation literature, always examine out-of-distribution cross-dataset generalization (e.g., training on Kvasir and testing on ETIS/ColonDB), boundary-sensitive metrics (Dice, mIoU), and practical computational complexity (FLOPs, parameters, inference latency).
