## A novel approach to overcome black box of AI for optical diagnosis in colonoscopy (DOI: 10.1038/s41598-025-04770-2)
**Core ML Technology:** An explainable artificial intelligence (XAI) framework named "niceAI" combining a deep convolutional neural network (ResNet-50 backbone trained with Adam optimizer, cosine annealing learning rate scheduler, cross-entropy loss, and Grad-CAM localized surrogate masking) with quantitative radiomics feature extraction (pyRadiomics v3.0.1 extracting GLRLM, GLCM, GLSZM texture and shape metrics) and clinical Narrow-Band Imaging International Colorectal Endoscopic (NICE) continuous 1–5 scoring. The system uses a multi-step feature selection pipeline consisting of Spearman's rank correlation filtering (|R| > 0.5, p < 0.05) followed by 100 iterations of 3-fold cross-validated linear regression to link intermediate deep convolutional features with human-interpretable radiomics and expert clinical scores for differentiating adenomatous (AD) from hyperplastic (HP) polyps.

## 1. The "Big Picture" & Motivation
* What is the main problem your research is trying to solve?
The research addresses the opaque "black box" nature of current deep learning computer-aided diagnosis (CADx) systems in colonoscopy optical diagnosis. While conventional deep learning models achieve high accuracy in distinguishing neoplastic from non-neoplastic polyps, their uninterpretable decision-making limits clinician trust, impedes error identification, and prevents broad clinical acceptance. Furthermore, many endoscopists (particularly novices) struggle with optical diagnosis, yet black-box AI tools have not conclusively demonstrated real-world clinical effectiveness in randomized controlled trials.

* What made you choose this specific topic?
Colorectal cancer (CRC) is the third most common cancer and second leading cause of cancer-related mortality worldwide. Accurate real-time optical diagnosis during colonoscopy (differentiating adenomatous polyps from hyperplastic polyps) can eliminate unnecessary polypectomies, lower pathological assessment costs, and avoid procedural complications (enabling "resect and discard" and "diagnose and leave" strategies). However, existing visual criteria (like the NICE classification) require extensive training, creating an urgent need for an AI assistive tool that mirrors clinicians' cognitive diagnostic reasoning.

* Why does this research matter for the industry right now?
Major gastrointestinal societies (ASGE and ESGE) have established strict quality benchmarks (PIVI and SODA) for optical diagnosis, but cost-saving paradigms cannot be implemented safely without reliable, trustworthy diagnostic tools. By providing an explainable AI system (niceAI) that achieves clinical benchmark performance while explicitly detailing surface and vessel pattern assessments aligned with the NICE classification, this research directly bridges AI predictions with clinical decision-making to facilitate real-world adoption.

## 2. The Practical Approach (Methodology)
* Are you doing mostly lab experiments, data analysis, or fieldwork?
This research consists of retrospective and prospective clinical data analysis and machine learning modeling using high-definition colonoscopy image and video datasets obtained from hospital patients at Seoul National University Hospital (SNUH) Healthcare System Gangnam Center.

* What framework or formulas are you using to analyze things?
The methodology employs PyTorch (v1.10.1) and Python 3.10 for deep learning (ResNet-50), pyRadiomics (v3.0.1) for texture/shape feature extraction, and Grad-CAM for surrogate mask generation within bounding boxes. Key statistical methods and formulas include:
- Spearman's rank correlation coefficient (|R| > 0.5, p < 0.05) to screen for significant relationships between non-interpretable deep features and interpretable radiomics features.
- Linear regression modeling and the coefficient of determination (R² score), Mean Absolute Error (MAE), Mean Squared Error (MSE), and Explained Variance Score (EVS) across 100 iterations with 3-fold cross-validation to select deep features that best predict continuous 1–5 NICE grading scores.
- Interpretable feature values calculated by multiplying the radiomics-to-deep feature regression coefficient by the deep-feature-to-NICE regression coefficient.
- Evaluation metrics including AUROC, Accuracy, Sensitivity, Specificity, PPV, NPV, Kendall's tau inter-rater/prediction correlation, and DeLong's test comparing niceAI against ResNet-50 and Vision Transformer (ViT) models.

* How do you usually collect your data or samples?
Data was gathered using high-definition Olympus colonoscopes (EVIS LUCERA CV 260SL/CV290SL) under an IRB-approved protocol with informed consent, structured into three distinct datasets:
- Dataset A (7,068 polyps: 5,492 adenomas, 1,576 hyperplastic) from the Gangnam-Real-Time Optical Diagnosis program, filtered to cases where expert optical diagnoses met PIVI thresholds and matched histology to eliminate pathological label noise for supervised ResNet-50 training.
- Dataset B (240 polyps: 120 adenomas, 120 hyperplastic) balanced and scored on a 1–5 continuous scale for NICE surface, vessel, and color criteria by three expert gastroenterologists (with 7, 14, and 20 years of experience) to train the explainable regression mapping.
- Dataset C (300 polyps: 160 adenomas, 140 hyperplastic) collected prospectively and temporally separated (July to December 2021) to evaluate real-world generalizability under realistic clinical prevalence ratios.

## 3. Obstacles & Breakthroughs
* What is the biggest challenge you've faced with this project?
The primary challenge was mathematically and conceptually aligning high-dimensional, abstract deep convolutional features (2,048 extracted features) with human-interpretable clinical criteria (the NICE classification) without sacrificing diagnostic accuracy, while navigating 15–20% baseline pathology misclassification rates in diminutive polyps and overcoming the labor-intensive requirement of multi-expert consensus scoring.

* Have you hit any dead ends or unexpected results yet?
Yes:
- Color features proved to be a dead end: zero color features were selected during screening because subtle color variations from endoscopic lighting angles, mucosal background shifts, and low clinical relevance made them unreliable.
- Feature overlap: Deep learning layers and radiomics features showed substantial overlap between surface patterns and vessel characteristics, indicating that radiomics features could not completely isolate surface from vascular cues.
- Standalone machine learning models relying solely on radiomics and color features hit a performance ceiling (AUROC < 0.900, Accuracy < 0.850), confirming that radiomics alone cannot match deep feature discriminative capacity.

* What has been your favorite finding or "eureka" moment?
The breakthrough finding was that niceAI matched the performance of opaque deep learning models (AUROC 0.946 vs. 0.956, Accuracy 0.883 vs. 0.877; no statistically significant difference via DeLong test, p > 0.05) and met SODA/PIVI 2 benchmarks while unveiling explicit, intuitive clinical connections. Specifically, `Original_glrlm_RunPercentage` directly captured texture uniformity (exhibiting opposing mathematical weights for uniform hyperplastic vs. heterogeneous adenomatous surfaces), and `original_glcm_Imc1` captured paired vascular pixel structures corresponding to adenomatous vessel patterns.

## 4. Lit Review & The "Gap"
* What major gap did you find in the previous research?
Prior deep learning CADx studies treated optical diagnosis as an uninterpretable binary classification task that outputs only diagnoses or confidence scores, without explaining the underlying morphology. Previous XAI attempts relying solely on heatmaps or isolated feature extraction failed to align internal representations with standardized clinical frameworks (like NICE) that endoscopists use during real-time procedures. Consequently, clinicians could not verify the AI's diagnostic reasoning, leading to inconclusive clinical trial results.

* Who is the go-to author or foundation paper in your field?
Foundation literature includes:
- Sano et al. (2013) and Hayashi et al. (2012) for the Narrow-Band Imaging International Colorectal Endoscopic (NICE) classification system.
- Rex et al. (ASGE PIVI statement, 2011/2015) and Kaminski et al. / ESGE (SODA statement) for defining optical diagnosis clinical efficacy thresholds.
- He et al. (2016) for the ResNet-50 deep learning architecture.
- Selvaraju et al. (2017) for Gradient-weighted Class Activation Mapping (Grad-CAM).
- van Griethuysen et al. (2017) for the pyRadiomics computational extraction platform.

* Did you find any major contradictions between previous studies?
Yes. While numerous retrospective studies reported high standalone CADx accuracy, prospective randomized controlled trials (RCTs) found that CADx did not consistently improve clinical outcomes in real-time colonoscopy. Furthermore, while classic endoscopic classification schemes (NICE) included lesion color as a diagnostic pillar, modern classifications (such as BASIC and JNET) and empirical radiomics analyses revealed that color features offer minimal discriminative value and high noise compared to texture and vascular morphology.

## 5. Future Impact & Advice
* What do you think is the next step for this research area?
Key next steps include:
1) Conducting prospective clinical trials within active endoscopic workflows to evaluate how explainable AI affects endoscopist decision-making, speed, and diagnostic accuracy, especially in borderline or ambiguous polyps.
2) Implementing formal multiple comparison corrections (e.g., False Discovery Rate or Bonferroni adjustments) for high-dimensional feature correlation screening.
3) Developing advanced architectures that more effectively disentangle overlapping surface and vessel representations.
4) Integrating real-time confidence calibration and interactive expert feedback mechanisms.

* Where do you see your findings being applied in real life?
In hospital endoscopy suites as an interactive, real-time CADx second reader integrated into colonoscopy video towers, displaying the predicted histological classification alongside quantitative surface and vessel pattern breakdown scores to give endoscopists the confidence to adopt "resect and discard" and "diagnose and leave" protocols in daily clinical practice.

* Any tips for someone trying to read papers in this domain?
1) Check whether an AI model meets formal medical society standards (e.g., ASGE PIVI negative predictive value > 90% and ESGE SODA sensitivity/specificity benchmarks) rather than relying solely on raw AUC or accuracy metrics.
2) Examine how the study addressed ground-truth label noise, given that diminutive polyp histopathology has an inherent 10–20% error rate.
3) Look beyond visual attention heatmaps (like Grad-CAM saliency) to see whether the model's intermediate features correspond to validated, standardized clinical classification systems (e.g., NICE, JNET, Paris) used by practicing clinicians.

## A hybrid compound scaling hypergraph neural network for robust cervical cancer subtype classification using whole slide cytology images

## Abstract

Cervical cancer is a major cause of mortality among women, particularly in low-income countries with insufficient screening programs. Manual cytological examination is time-consuming, error-prone and subject to inter-observer variability. Automated and robust classification of the whole slide cytology images for cervical cancer is essential for detecting precancerous and malignant lesions. We propose a novel deep learning framework, the Compound Scaling Hypergraph Neural Network model (CSHG-CervixNet), for robust classification of cervical cancer subtypes. The model integrates a Compound Scaling Convolutional Neural Network (CSCNN) with a k-dimensional Hypergraph Neural Network (kd-HGNN) architecture. CSCNN balances the network’s depth, width, and resolution, supporting effective feature representation with minimal computational overhead. kd-HGNN captures higher-order relationships between the features, and its propagation mechanism ensures better feature diffusion across distant nodes. The model is evaluated on the benchmark Sipakmed dataset and achieves an accuracy of 99.31%, with a macro-averaged precision of 98.97%, recall of 99.38%, and F1-score of 99.34%, demonstrating its robustness in cervical cancer subtype classification. Pathologists and other medical experts will find this study helpful in distinguishing cervical cancer subtypes so that targeted treatment may be provided and effective disease management is made possible.

## Introduction

Cervical Cancer (CC) ranks as the fourth most prevalent cancer affecting women globally and accounts for a significant portion of cancer-related deaths. According to recent statistics in 2020, it is estimated that Cervical Cancer caused over 600,000 new cases and about 340,000 deaths. This projection suggests that by 2030, Cervical cancer-related deaths could escalate to 400,000 people annually. According to CC cytology, the subtypes are commonly categorised into five distinct subtypes: Metaplastic, Dyskeratotic, Koilocytotic, Superficial-Intermediate and Parabasal. Accurate recognition and classification of these subtypes are crucial for cancer diagnosis and personalised treatments.

Traditionally, Cervical Cancer subtyping relies on manual analysis of WSIs by expert pathologists, which is error-prone, subjective and labour-intensive. Subtypes tend to have visual variations, which makes it challenging even for skilled pathologists to classify them. Furthermore, staining artifacts, overlapping cell features, and intra-class variations also make it more difficult for the classification task, which has the potential to cause diagnostic inaccuracies. Therefore, an automated Computer-Aided Diagnostic (CAD) tool to classify cervical cancer subtypes from WSIs is essential for improving accuracy, reducing workload, and facilitating early and precise treatment planning.

Many deep-learning techniques have shown considerable promise in detecting various cancers, such as lung, breast, cervical, and colorectal. In recent studies, Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs) are the basis of several CAD systems for evaluating cervical cytology images, significantly increasing diagnostic efficiency and precision. However, CNNs effectively extract discriminative features; they often struggle to capture complex, multi-relational interactions among features. Transformer-based architecture has recently gained attention for its powerful self-attention mechanisms in modelling global contexts, especially for medical image applications. They are associated with drawbacks such as model complexity, large data requirements, and lack of interpretability in resource-poor clinical environments. Even though they have demonstrated promising results, they likely lack fine-grained, higher-order feature modelling capabilities, which are crucial for tasks such as cytological subtype classification.

To address the limitations of existing approaches, recent work has explored Graph Convolutional Networks (GCN), which have sought to reduce the inherent shortfalls of traditional approaches by adopting pairwise interactions. However, standard GCNs are restricted from operating on pairwise (2-node) connections, whereas Hypergraph Neural Networks (HGNNs) have gained increased attention by enabling multi-way relational modelling through hyperedges. However, many conventional HGNN structures still use static structures and cannot handle higher-order correlations in large, heterogeneous datasets. A hybrid subtype classification framework, Compound Scaling Hypergraph neural network (CSHG-CervixNet), is proposed to overcome these issues. The model utilises efficient Compound Scaling Convolutional Neural Network (CSCNN) deep feature extraction and modified k-dimensional Hypergraph Neural Network (kd-HGNN) for robust classification. We adopt CSCNN as a backbone for feature extraction, which uniformly scales depth, width and resolution, enabling the model to extract deep, multi-scale features from whole slide cytology images. The kd-HGNN models higher-order relationships of the extracted features through hypergraph construction based on neighbourhood similarity, ensuring comprehensive feature aggregation and precise classification. By embedding a feature propagation mechanism in the hypergraph architecture, we effectively model the diffusion of feature information between local and global relational contexts to capture inter-cellular dependencies inherent in cytological images more accurately. Leveraging the publicly available SipakMed dataset, our model is meticulously designed to address the nuanced morphological variances across subtypes, ensuring accuracy and scalability.

### Contributions
1. **Compound Scaling Convolutional Neural Network for deep feature extraction**: CSCNN is utilised to extract the deep features from cervical whole slide cytology images. The uniform scaling of depth, width and resolution ensures an efficient feature extraction process, capturing intricate morphological variations crucial for subtype classification.
2. **Development of K-dimensional Hypergraph Neural Network for classification**: A robust kd-HGNN architecture that effectively models higher-order relationships among the extracted feature vectors. The hyperedges are based on neighbourhood similarity. The kd-HGNN facilitates comprehensive feature aggregation, thereby enhancing classification accuracy.
3. **A comprehensive evaluation of the SipakMed dataset**: Extensive experiments are conducted on the benchmark SipakMed cervical cytology dataset. The results demonstrate that the proposed hybrid framework outperforms the baseline methods in cervical cancer subtype classification tasks.

## Related works

Machine Learning (ML) and Deep Learning (DL) techniques have significantly advanced cervical cancer diagnosis. These techniques have been used to improve automated diagnostic models’ accuracy, dependability, and interpretability while addressing issues including cytological image variability, the requirement for strong feature extraction and robust classification. This section examines the existing studies in three main areas: (1) ML approaches, which prioritise feature engineering and conventional classification algorithms; (2) DL methods, which employ CNNs and complex architectures to achieve increased accuracy and generalizability; and (3) ensemble and hybrid models, which integrate various approaches to enhance performance and robustness. This review contextualises and illustrates the development of techniques by evaluating various approaches.

[Summary of literature review...]

## Proposed CSHG-CervixNet architecture

The proposed hybrid model CSHG-CervixNet is trained and validated using the SipakMed cervical cancer cytology images. The model integrates a Compound Scaling Convolutional Neural Network (CSCNN) for feature extraction and a k-dimensional-based Hypergraph Neural Network (kd-HGNN) for robust classification.

## Data set description

The SipakMed Database contains 4049 images featuring individual cells extracted from 966 group cell images obtained from whole slide images. These images are captured using a CCD camera mounted with an optical microscope. The cell images are sorted into five groups, including normal, abnormal, and benign.

## Feature extraction using compound scaling convolutional neural network (CSCNN)

The histopathological images from the SipakMed dataset serve as input, where deep features are extracted using a Compound Scaling Convolutional Neural Network. Unlike existing deeper CNN architectures like DenseNet ResNet, our feature extraction model utilises a compound scaling method. The Compound scaling method scales the network’s depth ($\widehat{d}$), width ($\widehat{w}$), and resolution ($\widehat{r}$) uniformly using a compound coefficient ($d$).

Equations:
1. $Depth: \widehat{d} = \alpha^{\varnothing}$
2. $Width: \widehat{w} = \beta^{\varnothing}$
3. $Resolution: \widehat{r} = \gamma^{\varnothing}$

The core building block of the CSCNN is a Mobile Inverted Bottleneck Convolution Block (MBConv block), which integrates five essential operations: depth-wise convolution, Projection, Squeeze and Excitation (SE) module, Expansion, and Swish Activation.

## K-dimensional hypergraph neural network (kd-HGNN) with propagation for classification

The features extracted by CSCNN are subsequently processed by kd-HGNN. Relationships between sets of objects are represented by hypergraphs, which makes it possible to simulate complex interactions in a variety of fields.

The hypergraph convolution can be formulated as:
$X^{l+1} = \sigma(D_{v}^{-\frac{1}{2}} H W D_{e}^{-1} H^{T} D_{v}^{-\frac{1}{2}} X^{l} \Theta^{l})$

## ColoViT: a synergistic integration of EfficientNet and vision transformers for advanced colon cancer detection (DOI: 10.1007/s00432-025-06199-6)
**Core ML Technology:** Hybrid deep learning ensemble framework integrating Convolutional Neural Networks (**EfficientNet V2 B0**) with self-attention architectures (**Vision Transformer / ViT-B16**) via **Geometric Mean Ensembling** (weighted soft voting with weights $w_E = 0.3$ and $w_V = 0.7$). EfficientNet V2 B0 utilizes MBConv and Fused-MBConv blocks with Squeeze-and-Excitation (SE) channel recalibration for fine-grained local feature extraction, while ViT-B16 utilizes multi-head self-attention ($Q, K, V$ projections) for capturing global contextual dependencies across image patches. Training employs transfer learning with symmetric cross-entropy loss, Adam/Rectified Adam optimizer, learning rate of 0.0001, batch size of 16/32, dropout (0.3), L2 weight decay ($1 \times 10^{-5}$), 5-fold stratified cross-validation, and extensive image preprocessing/augmentation (median filtering, CLAHE, gamma correction, unsharp masking, elastic deformations). Implemented in PyTorch 3.7 on an Intel Xeon E5-2780 CPU and NVIDIA GeForce RTX 1080 GPU.

## 1. The "Big Picture" & Motivation
* What is the main problem your research is trying to solve?
Colorectal Carcinoma (CRCA) is one of the leading causes of cancer-related mortality globally, where survival rates drop drastically from 91% for localized disease down to 13% once distant metastasis occurs. While early detection and removal of precancerous polyps and dysplastic lesions significantly improves survival, conventional colonoscopy and histopathology examinations are physically demanding, time-consuming, and subject to high miss rates and diagnostic error caused by visual fatigue, small lesion size, and subtle morphological differences between benign and malignant tissues. This research aims to eliminate missed detections and provide automated, precise, multi-class polyp classification and dysplasia grading.

* What made you choose this specific topic?
The stark clinical contrast between early-stage curability and late-stage lethality motivated this work. Furthermore, existing deep learning models in digital pathology either rely exclusively on CNNs (which struggle to capture long-range contextual relationships) or standalone Vision Transformers (which lack inductive bias for local textural details and require massive training data). Combining both paradigms specifically targets the challenge of fine-grained histopathological grading across six distinct tissue classes.

* Why does this research matter for the industry right now?
Digital pathology and computer-aided diagnosis (CAD) systems are rapidly transitioning from experimental tools to clinical integration. A diagnostic framework that achieves 99.4% micro-average accuracy, 98.4% F1-score, and sub-15ms inference latency per patch provides the industry with a practical, highly consistent decision-support tool to standardize colorectal polyp grading, reduce inter-observer variability among pathologists, and streamline clinical screening workflows.

## 2. The Practical Approach (Methodology)
* Are you doing mostly lab experiments, data analysis, or fieldwork?
The study focuses on computational data analysis, digital pathology image processing, and deep learning model benchmarking using high-resolution histological image datasets.

* What framework or formulas are you using to analyze things?
The implementation is built on Python 3.7 using PyTorch (Paszke et al. 2019) and scikit-learn (v1.2.2). Key mathematical formulations and frameworks include:
- **Vision Transformer Self-Attention:**
  $$\text{Attention}(Q, K, V) = \text{softmax}\left(\frac{QK^T}{\sqrt{d_k}}\right)V$$
  where $Q = XW_Q$, $K = XW_K$, and $V = XW_V$.
- **Geometric Mean Ensembling / Weighted Soft Voting:**
  $$P(i) = P_E(i)^{w_E} \cdot P_V(i)^{w_V}$$
  $$\hat{y} = \arg\max_i \left( P(i) \right)$$
  with empirically optimized weights $w_E = 0.3$ (EfficientNet V2 B0) and $w_V = 0.7$ (ViT-B16).
- **Statistical and Diagnostic Metrics:** Accuracy, Precision, Recall, F1-score, Specificity ($\frac{TN}{TN+FP}$), Matthews Correlation Coefficient (MCC), Cohen’s Kappa ($\kappa = \frac{p_o - p_e}{1 - p_e}$), Youden’s Index ($J = \text{Sensitivity} + \text{Specificity} - 1$), and One-vs-Rest (OvR) ROC-AUC.
- **Image Preprocessing Pipeline:** Bilinear interpolation (224×224), Min-Max normalization, 3×3 median filtering, gamma correction ($\gamma = 1.2$), Contrast Limited Adaptive Histogram Equalization (CLAHE: clip limit 2.0, tile 8×8), 5×5 Gaussian blur, and 3×3 unsharp masking sharpening.

* How do you usually collect your data or samples?
Data was sourced from established, expert-annotated histopathology benchmark repositories:
1. **UniToPatho Dataset (Primary Benchmark):** 9,536 annotated H&E-stained colorectal patches derived from 292 whole-slide images (IEEE DataPort), categorized into 6 classes: Normal tissue (NORM), Hyperplastic Polyp (HP), Tubular Adenoma High-Grade (TA.HG), Tubular Adenoma Low-Grade (TA.LG), Tubulo-Villous Adenoma High-Grade (TVA.HG), and Tubulo-Villous Adenoma Low-Grade (TVA.LG).
2. **NCT-CRC-HE-100K Dataset (External Validation):** An independent dataset containing 100,000 H&E colorectal histology patches from a separate institution with distinct staining protocols.
3. **Data Augmentation:** Random rotations ($0^\circ–360^\circ$), horizontal/vertical flips, random zoom ($0.8–1.2$), HSV brightness scaling, LAB contrast scaling, Gaussian noise injection, and elastic transformations simulating biological tissue deformations.

## 3. Obstacles & Breakthroughs
* What is the biggest challenge you've faced with this project?
The primary challenge was accurately distinguishing between closely related dysplasia grades with high inter-class visual similarity—notably differentiating Tubular Adenoma High-Grade (TA.HG) from Low-Grade (TA.LG) due to overlapping glandular architecture and ambiguous dysplastic features, as well as separating Hyperplastic Polyps (HP) from Normal tissue (NORM) under varying staining intensities and class imbalances.

* Have you hit any dead ends or unexpected results yet?
Standalone baseline models hit performance ceilings: standalone EfficientNet V2 B0 and ViT-B16 exhibited elevated misclassification rates on borderline dysplastic lesions. Additionally, ensemble ensembling introduced an inference latency penalty (0.0147 s/sample vs 0.0059 s for EfficientNet alone—a ~2.5× computational slowdown), which presents a potential bottleneck for deployment on edge/embedded endoscope hardware without GPU acceleration. Furthermore, near-perfect ROC-AUC values under the One-vs-Rest strategy can mask class imbalance effects if not corroborated by class-wise MCC and Kappa statistics.

* What has been your favorite finding or "eureka" moment?
The discovery that synergistically fusing the local textural feature extraction of EfficientNet with the global spatial self-attention of ViT via geometric mean weighting ($0.3 / 0.7$) resolved borderline dysplastic ambiguities, pushing overall accuracy to 99.4% and achieving statistically significant improvements ($p < 0.01$, paired t-test). Crucially, the model maintained 97.1% accuracy and 98.4% AUC on an external, multi-center dataset (NCT-CRC-HE-100K) without any fine-tuning or retraining, validating its generalizability.

## 4. Lit Review & The "Gap"
* What major gap did you find in the previous research?
Prior research primarily focused on binary classification (polyp vs. non-polyp) or coarse detection, lacking comprehensive, fine-grained multi-class grading across clinical dysplasia stages (NORM, HP, TA.LG, TA.HG, TVA.LG, TVA.HG). Furthermore, earlier CNN-based methods suffered from limited receptive fields, while pure Transformer architectures lacked localized spatial inductive bias and struggled on small-to-medium histopathology cohorts without cross-institutional validation.

* Who is the go-to author or foundation paper in your field?
- **UniToPatho Benchmark:** Bertero et al. (2021) for establishing the 6-class colorectal histopathology dataset.
- **Efficient Architectures:** Tan and Le (2021) for EfficientNetV2; Dosovitskiy et al. (2020) and Vaswani et al. (2017) for Vision Transformers and multi-head self-attention.
- **Deep Learning in Colorectal Polyp Detection:** Korbar et al. (2017), Kather et al. (2019), and Pacal et al. (2021, 2025) for foundational work in polyp classification, WSI tissue profiling, and hybrid CNN-ViT architectures.

* Did you find any major contradictions between previous studies?
A notable divergence exists between reported laboratory accuracy and clinical translation: some prior AI systems (e.g., Quan et al. 2022) demonstrated increased polyp detection rates during clinical trials that nonetheless failed to reach statistical significance. In addition, standalone CNNs versus pure ViTs showed conflicting trade-offs between local texture discrimination and broad contextual awareness, proving that neither architecture in isolation is optimal for multi-grade histopathology.

## 5. Future Impact & Advice
* What do you think is the next step for this research area?
Future directions include applying model compression, pruning, 8-bit/4-bit quantization, and knowledge distillation to shrink the ensemble architecture for real-time edge computing on endoscope video feeds. Other next steps include expanding validation to multi-ethnic, multi-center cohorts, incorporating domain-knowledge-guided attention maps, and scaling the framework to whole-slide image (WSI) level gigapixel processing.

* Where do you see your findings being applied in real life?
Direct integration into hospital Picture Archiving and Communication Systems (PACS) and digital pathology laboratory platforms, functioning as an automated real-time triage and second-opinion system to flag high-risk dysplastic lesions, prioritize pathologist review queues, and minimize diagnostic errors.

* Any tips for someone trying to read papers in this domain?
Focus on how models handle the trade-off between local cellular texture (extracted by CNN inductive biases) and global tissue architecture (captured by Transformer self-attention). Never evaluate models solely on aggregate accuracy—scrutinize class-wise confusion matrices, MCC, Cohen's Kappa, and external multi-center cross-validation results to assess true clinical robustness against staining and institutional variations.

## Artificial Intelligence in Inflammatory Bowel Disease Endoscopy – A Review of Current Evidence and a Critical Perspective on Future Challenges (DOI: 10.1177/17562848251350896)
**Core ML Technology:** Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs) for spatial feature extraction in endoscopic still images and video frames; Recurrent Neural Networks (RNNs/CRNNs, e.g., UC-Dense-Net) integrating temporal video dynamics; Vision Transformers (ViTs) with self-attention for global contextual image analysis; Self-Supervised Learning (SSL) foundation models (e.g., DINOv2 in the Certai framework); Computer-Aided Detection (CADe) and Characterization (CADx) systems (e.g., EndoBRAIN, EndoBRAIN EYE); Red Density (RD) color-isolation algorithms; Generative Adversarial Networks (GANs) for synthetic data generation; and Multimodal/Fusion architectures combining video endoscopy, histology, and clinical/biochemical biomarkers.

## 1. The "Big Picture" & Motivation
* What is the main problem your research is trying to solve?
Endoscopic evaluation in inflammatory bowel disease (IBD)—encompassing ulcerative colitis (UC) and Crohn’s disease (CD)—currently relies on subjective visual assessment by individual endoscopists. This introduces substantial inter-observer variability (especially in mild-to-moderate disease and remission), diagnostic delays, inconsistencies in disease activity scoring (e.g., Mayo Endoscopic Subscore [MES], UCEIS), missed dysplastic lesions, and challenges in clinical trial reproducibility and real-time treatment monitoring.

* What made you choose this specific topic?
IBD is a chronic, relapsing immune-mediated disorder with rising global prevalence where achieving mucosal and histological healing is critical to prevent hospitalizations, colectomies, and colorectal cancer. Gastrointestinal endoscopy produces massive volumes of rich visual data, yet traditional scoring systems are rigid, subjective, and prone to high inter-reader disagreement (e.g., only 27% agreement for endoscopic remission and 37% for moderate disease), creating an ideal domain for AI and deep learning to standardize assessment and automate complex diagnostic tasks.

* Why does this research matter for the industry right now?
In clinical trials, central reading is expensive, slow, and imperfect; AI-based cumulative scoring can reduce sample size requirements by up to 50% (e.g., in trials like UNIFI) while increasing statistical power and sensitivity to therapeutic response. In clinical practice and medical device development, real-time AI tools (CADe/CADx) promise standardized disease severity grading, non-invasive histological activity prediction, rapid capsule endoscopy triage (e.g., reducing review time to under 4 minutes), and early detection of subtle colitis-associated dysplasia.

## 2. The Practical Approach (Methodology)
* Are you doing mostly lab experiments, data analysis, or fieldwork?
This work is a comprehensive systematic review and critical synthesis of clinical studies, data analysis pipelines, and machine learning models applied to endoscopic datasets (retrospective and prospective cohort studies, multicenter clinical trial datasets, video colonoscopy recordings, capsule endoscopy streams, and ultra-magnification endocytoscopy).

* What framework or formulas are you using to analyze things?
- Deep learning architectures including CNNs (e.g., ResNet, DenseNet variants), Recurrent Neural Networks (e.g., UC-Dense-Net for temporal modeling), and Vision Transformers (ViTs) pre-trained with self-supervised frameworks (DINOv2 in Certai).
- Computational image processing algorithms such as the Red Density (RD) score algorithm to quantify mucosal hypervascularization without human training inputs.
- Novel scoring formulations including the Cumulative Disease Score (CDS) which sums segmental inflammatory contributions across the colon, and automated continuous scoring scales (0–10).
- Standard clinical reference scoring indices including MES (0–3), UCEIS (0–8), PICaSSO score, Robarts Histopathology Index (RHI), Geboes score, and Lewis score for capsule endoscopy.
- Statistical metrics: Cohen’s/quadratic weighted kappa (κ), intraclass correlation coefficients (ICC), receiver operating characteristic area under the curve (ROC-AUC), sensitivity, specificity, positive predictive value (PPV), and negative predictive value (NPV).

* How do you usually collect your data or samples?
Datasets are acquired from standard white-light colonoscopies, high-definition virtual chromoendoscopy (VCE), dye-based chromoendoscopy, ultra-magnified endocytoscopy (up to 520x magnification with methylene blue/crystal violet staining), wireless pan-enteric capsule endoscopy (CE), and intestinal ultrasound (IUS). Datasets span curated still image repositories (e.g., 16,514 to 40,758 images) to large multicenter clinical trial video databases (e.g., UC-SCALE trained on 4,326 sigmoidoscopy videos from 1,953 UC patients across 554 clinical sites in 5 clinical trials) with automated frame-quality filtering to remove artifacts (bubbles, debris, motion blur).

## 3. Obstacles & Breakthroughs
* What is the biggest challenge you've faced with this project?
The biggest challenges include: (1) high inter-observer variability in ground-truth labeling, particularly for mild-to-moderate inflammation; (2) selection bias in still-image datasets that omit real-world artifacts (debris, poor lighting, motion blur); (3) subtle, flat morphology and inflammatory background noise that obscure colitis-associated dysplasia; (4) lack of external multicenter validation across diverse endoscope hardware; and (5) black-box opacity and differing international regulatory frameworks (e.g., EU MDR/AI Act vs. US FDA).

* Have you hit any dead ends or unexpected results yet?
- Predicting long-term clinical relapse/disease flares over extended periods (e.g., 5-year prediction) using baseline endoscopic imaging alone yielded disappointing results (e.g., Red Density score achieving an AUC of only 0.68), illustrating that single-timepoint visual metrics cannot capture multi-factorial systemic flare dynamics.
- Models trained strictly on sporadic non-IBD polyps failed drastically when applied to IBD dysplasia (yielding only ~50% sensitivity), proving that general CADe systems cannot be directly repurposed for colitis surveillance without specialized retraining on inflamed, flat mucosa.
- AI still struggles to outperform experienced gastroenterologists in differentiating IBD from non-IBD mimics (e.g., infectious or ischemic colitis).

* What has been your favorite finding or "eureka" moment?
- The Cumulative Disease Score (CDS) breakthrough: aggregating AI-scored inflammation across all colonic segments captured longitudinal treatment responsiveness so effectively that it resulted in a 50% reduction in the clinical trial sample size needed to detect therapeutic efficacy (demonstrated on the UNIFI ustekinumab trial dataset).
- AI models trained purely on endoscopic video frames successfully predicting microscopic histological activity/remission (Geboes/RHI) with >90% accuracy, demonstrating cross-endpoint inference without invasive biopsies.
- Real-time video analysis frameworks (e.g., UC-SCALE, Gottlieb et al., Takenaka et al.) and AI-assisted capsule endoscopy (e.g., AXARO) slashing video review times to under 4 minutes while achieving a 97% negative predictive value.

## 4. Lit Review & The "Gap"
* What major gap did you find in the previous research?
Prior research predominantly relied on binary classification (remission vs. active disease) on clean, pre-selected still images from single centers, ignoring the continuous spectrum of severity and real-world video artifacts. Furthermore, research heavily skewed toward UC, leaving a major gap in AI applications for conventional ileo-colonoscopy in Crohn’s disease (due to its transmural, patchy nature) and a distinct lack of validated CADe/CADx tools specifically built for colitis-associated dysplasia surveillance and non-IBD mimic differentiation.

* Who is the go-to author or foundation paper in your field?
- Stidham et al. (2019) – Published the pioneering proof-of-concept CNN study in IBD endoscopy (MES classification) and developed the Cumulative Disease Score (CDS).
- Takenaka et al. (2020, 2021) – Developed large-scale neural network systems for UCEIS remission and histological prediction, advancing to full prospective multicenter video colonoscopy models.
- Gottlieb et al. (2021) – Demonstrated real-time automated video colonoscopy evaluation for UC severity.
- Maeda et al. & Kudo et al. – Pioneered ultra-magnification endocytoscopy AI systems (EndoBRAIN / EndoBRAIN EYE).

* Did you find any major contradictions between previous studies?
Contradictions emerged regarding reference standard efficacy: models trained using more granular scoring systems like UCEIS and PICaSSO consistently outperformed models trained on the simpler Mayo Endoscopic Subscore (MES) (meta-analysis sensitivity: 93.6% for UCEIS vs. 82.0% for MES), challenging the traditional clinical preference for MES. Additionally, while early single-center still-image studies reported near-perfect accuracies (>95%), multi-center real-world video and multi-capsule platform studies demonstrated slight drops in sensitivity (82–94.6%) and accuracy (86.1%), exposing the hidden impact of dataset selection bias and cross-device hardware discrepancies.

## 5. Future Impact & Advice
* What do you think is the next step for this research area?
The field is moving toward: (1) Self-Supervised Learning (SSL) and Vision Transformers (ViTs) (e.g., DINOv2 / Certai) to leverage massive unlabelled video libraries without manual annotation bottlenecks; (2) Multimodal "Fusion Models" integrating video endoscopy, histopathology, intestinal ultrasound, laboratory biomarkers (albumin, CRP, fecal calprotectin), and multi-omics; (3) Federated Learning architectures to train across global hospital networks while strictly preserving patient privacy; and (4) Prospective clinical trials testing AI-driven real-time dysplasia surveillance and treat-to-target guidance.

* Where do you see your findings being applied in real life?
- Centralized automated reading in IBD clinical trials to reduce operational costs, eliminate reader bias, and lower patient enrollment requirements.
- Real-time CADe/CADx endoscopic overlays in community and academic endoscopy suites to assist gastroenterologists in grading mucosal inflammation, targeting biopsies, and detecting flat dysplasia.
- Automated capsule endoscopy screening triage to rapidly rule out small bowel Crohn’s disease within minutes in outpatient clinics.

* Any tips for someone trying to read papers in this domain?
Scrutinize the input data type (still images vs. full unedited video), the reference ground truth (MES vs. UCEIS vs. histology), and whether the study performed external multicenter validation on independent hardware platforms. Beware of inflated accuracy claims from small, pre-curated datasets that eliminate blurry or obscured frames, and check whether the model addresses multiclass severity or merely binary remission classification.

## Pathological omics prediction of early and advanced colon cancer based on artificial intelligence model

## Abstract
Artificial intelligence (AI) models based on pathological slides have great potential to assist pathologists in disease diagnosis and have become an important research direction in the field of medical image analysis. The aim of this study was to develop an AI model based on whole-slide images to predict the stage of colon cancer. In this study, a total of 100 pathological slides of colon cancer patients were collected as the training set, and 421 pathological slides of colon cancer were downloaded from The Cancer Genome Atlas (TCGA) database as the external validation set. Cellprofiler and CLAM tools were used to extract pathological features, and machine learning algorithms and deep learning algorithms were used to construct prediction models. The area under the curve (AUC) of the best machine learning model was 0.78 in the internal test set and 0.68 in the external test set. The AUC of the deep learning model in the internal test set was 0.889, and the accuracy of the model was 0.854. The AUC of the deep learning model in the external test set was 0.700. The prediction model has the potential to generalize in the process of combining pathological omics diagnosis. Compared with machine learning, deep learning has higher recognition and accuracy of images, and the performance of the model is better.

## Introduction
Colon cancer is one of the common gastrointestinal malignancies, accounting for approximately 10% of all cancers and cancer-related deaths diagnosed annually worldwide. Although mortality from colon cancer continues to decline overall, colon cancer is rapidly shifting diagnosis at a younger age, at a more advanced stage, and in the left colon/rectum. Early diagnosis of colon cancer can greatly improve treatment outcomes. Common diagnostic methods include colonoscopy, histopathological examination and imaging examination. Traditional pathological classification methods rely on the experience of pathologists and observation under the microscope, which is not only time-consuming but also easily affected by subjective judgment. Therefore, the development of a rapid, objective and accurate automated pathological classification method is essential to improve the treatment outcome of colon cancer patients.

The spatial characteristics of different tissues in histopathological images play an important role in the diagnosis and prognosis of cancer. Pathological cancer tissue slide images from hematoxylin and eosin (H&E) staining, containing information about the morphology and spatial context of different cell types coexisting in the tumor microenvironment. Traditionally, pathologists identify and distinguish the different pathological subtypes of colon cancer by visual inspection of histopathological slides. However, this method is time-consuming, laborious and cumbersome. To overcome these limitations, researchers have turned to deep learning-based approaches. The Clustering-constrained Attention Multiple Instance Learning (CLAM) algorithm is an interpretable weakly supervised deep learning method specifically designed for processing Whole-slide images (WSIs) of pathology.

The aim of this study is to construct a colon cancer grading prediction model based on histopathological slides data to provide pathologists with more accurate support during routine diagnosis.

## Materials and methods

### Data collection
A total of 103 pathology slides of colon cancer patients in the Third Affiliated Hospital of Heilongjiang University of Chinese Medicine from 2020 to 2023 were collected as in-house data. 100 cases were randomly assigned to the training and test sets (47 early, 53 advanced). 421 colon cancer pathological sections (TCGA-COAD) were downloaded from The Cancer Genome Atlas (TCGA). Among them, 75 early and 346 advanced.

### A network framework to predict tumor stage
In this study, machine learning algorithms and deep learning algorithms were used to construct colon cancer grading prediction models. Shapley Additive exPlanations (SHAP) was used to account for the importance of features. In addition, we used deep learning model ResNet50 to extract features, then added multi-layer neural network to output patch prediction results, and finally output the prediction results of the whole slice by average pooling.

### Machine learning training

#### Data preprocessing
H&E-stained pathological sections were cut using OpenSlide-Python at a magnification of 20×, and 10 512 × 512 tiles were selected from each section as representative tiles. The color of the tiles was normalized using the vahadane method.

#### CellProfiler extracts cell features
The selected tiles were subjected to feature extraction using cellprofiler, and the H&E-stained images were firstly divided into hematoxylin-stained and eosin-stained grey-scale images. Then the images were automatically segmented to identify the nucleus and cytoplasm and extract quantitative image features such as object shape, size, texture, granularity, pixel intensity distribution, etc.

#### Feature selection
Least Absolute Shrinkage and Selection Operator (LASSO) regression and Recursive Feature Elimination (RFE) were used to screen features.

#### Construction of machine learning models
Multiple machine learning algorithms including XGBoost, LightGBMXT, CatBoost, LightGBMLarge, ExtraTrees, RandomForest, and KNeighbors were integrated to train the model.

#### Feature importance analysis
The importance of features is explained using SHAP.

### Deep learning training

#### Pathological image segmentation and feature extraction
The Python-based CLAM analysis tool was used to cut the pathology slides into non-overlapping tiles with 256 × 256 pixels for feature extraction. Then, the feature vectors are extracted by feeding the cropped patches to the pre-trained deep network ResNet50.

#### Construction of deep learning models
The deep learning model was trained using the multi-instance learning algorithm based on the attention mechanism integrated in CLAM.

#### Attention heatmaps generation
Attention scores were calculated and saved for all patches, converted to percentiles, and mapped to the corresponding positions of the pathological images.

### Statistical analysis
All analyses were performed with the use of R (version 4.3.3) or Python (version 3.10.14). Pearson correlation was used to analyze the correlation between characteristics. Significance was considered at p < 0.05.

## Results

### Screening of pathological features
LASSO and RFE regression analysis yielded 46 features representing 3 types: Intensity Texture features of the nucleoplasm on Hematoxylin images, Intensity Texture features of the nucleoplasm on Texture features of nucleoplasm on Eosin images, and image level features.

### Performance of machine learning models
In the internal test set, the RandomForest model performed best with an AUC of 0.78.

### Generalization ability of machine learning models
The AUC of the model in the external data set (TCGA-COAD) reached 0.68.

### SHAP feature importance analysis
The results showed that the Granularity, Intensity features of hematoxylin-stained nucleus region were more important for the model performance.

### Performance of deep learning models
The deep learning model achieved an AUC of 0.889 and an accuracy of 0.854 in the internal test set.

### Transfer efficiency of deep learning model
The AUC of the deep learning model on the external test set reached 0.700.

### Attention map generation
Attention heatmaps were generated to account for the relative importance of the regions in the WSI. These heat maps show the prediction results of different pathological stages of colon cancer at the plaque level.

## Discussion
This study demonstrated the effectiveness of machine learning and deep learning algorithms in grading colon cancer patients. In this study, the random forest classifier showed the greatest potential in terms of accuracy and AUC, indicating that the machine learning model based on big data has a better predictive ability. In addition, using deep learning algorithm for training, the model achieved an AUC of 0.889 and an accuracy of 0.854 in the internal test set, while the AUC of the model in the external test set was 0.700. However, the results also revealed some limitations, such as differences in data sources and sample size. Future research directions can focus on further optimization of the model by incorporating multimodal data.

## Conclusion
In summary, this study demonstrated the potential of deep learning and machine learning in the pathological grading of colon cancer and highlighted the importance of automated pathological classification methods to improve the diagnostic efficiency.

## Colorectal cancer unmasked: A synergistic AI framework for Hyper-granular image dissection, precision segmentation, and automated diagnosis** (DOI: [10.1186/s12880-025-01826-7](https://doi.org/10.1186/s12880-025-01826-7))
**Core ML Technology:** A synergistic two-stage Computer-Aided Diagnosis (CADx) system integrating Vision Transformers (ViTs) for global contextual lesion classification and DeepLabV3+ (with a ResNet-50 backbone and Atrous Spatial Pyramid Pooling [ASPP]) for high-precision semantic segmentation. The pipeline incorporates Principal Component Analysis (PCA) retaining 95% variance for dimensionality reduction, median filtering denoising, stratified sampling, and targeted data augmentation, evaluated against CNN baselines (ResNet-50, DenseNet-201, VGG-16) and deployed via Google Cloud.

## 1. The "Big Picture" & Motivation
* What is the main problem your research is trying to solve?
Colorectal cancer (CRC) represents the third most common malignancy and the second deadliest cause of cancer-related mortality globally (~1.9 million new cases and ~10 million deaths annually). Conventional colonoscopy screening relies heavily on endoscopist visual acuity and manual inspection, leading to human error, inter-observer variability, and missed precancerous polyps or incipient lesions. Current computer-aided diagnosis (CADx) systems face limitations such as excessive computational overhead, training on isolated/non-generalizable datasets, and an inability to simultaneously capture long-range contextual relationships and sharp, high-resolution lesion boundaries. This research develops an automated, interpretable, and computationally viable CADx system for hyper-granular classification and pixel-level segmentation of colorectal abnormalities.

* What made you choose this specific topic?
CRC typically develops slowly from benign precancerous polyps into invasive carcinomas over several years, offering a critical window for early clinical intervention and curative endoscopic resection. However, incidence rates among younger populations are rising by 1–2% annually, and significant geographical disparities exist in screening infrastructure and diagnostic awareness (e.g., higher awareness in western/southern India such as Maharashtra and Kerala compared to limited diagnostic resources in northern and eastern states). Developing an automated, reproducible AI diagnostic assistant addresses these screening gaps and democratizes early detection.

* Why does this research matter for the industry right now?
Modern endoscopic procedures generate immense volumes of motion video and high-resolution image frames, creating an overwhelming workload for clinical specialists. Integrating a cloud-connected (Google Cloud Storage), real-time CADx system standardizes diagnostic quality, minimizes false negatives during live colonoscopy, expedites clinical decision-making for biopsies or surgical resections, and provides scalable diagnostic assistance to resource-constrained medical centers worldwide.

## 2. The Practical Approach (Methodology)
* Are you doing mostly lab experiments, data analysis, or fieldwork?
In silico computational experimentation and deep learning data analysis. The study evaluates deep learning and transformer models on a large-scale curated benchmark dataset of clinical colonoscopy images (CKHK-22) using high-performance computing workstations (HP Z4 Workstation with an Intel Xeon W-2133 CPU, 64 GB DDR4 ECC RAM, NVIDIA Quadro P5000 16 GB GPU, Ubuntu 20.04 LTS) and Google Colab Pro+ with GPU acceleration.

* What framework or formulas are you using to analyze things?
* **Frameworks & Tooling:** Python 3.7, TensorFlow 2.11, Keras backend, OpenCV, NumPy, Pandas, Matplotlib, and Seaborn.
* **Architectures & Hyperparameters:**
  * *Vision Transformer (ViT):* Linear patch projection, positional embeddings, multi-head self-attention (8 attention heads), feed-forward dimension 2048, learning rate 0.0001, batch size 16, dropout 0.1, 5 epochs, optimized with Adam and L2 weight regularization.
  * *DeepLabV3+:* ResNet-50 encoder with atrous/dilated convolutions, Atrous Spatial Pyramid Pooling (ASPP) module, decoder upsampling with multi-head spatial attention, learning rate 0.0001, batch size 16, dropout 0.3, 30 epochs, Adam optimizer, early stopping (patience = 3 epochs).
  * *CNN Baselines:* ResNet-50, DenseNet-201, and VGG-16 trained with learning rate 0.001, batch size 16, dropout 0.3, 10 epochs, Adam optimizer.
* **Formulas & Evaluation Metrics:**
  * *Dimensionality Reduction:* Principal Component Analysis (PCA) retaining 95% of cumulative data variance.
  * *Classification Metrics:* Accuracy ($\frac{TP+TN}{TP+TN+FP+FN}$), Precision ($\frac{TP}{TP+FP}$), Recall/Sensitivity ($\frac{TP}{TP+FN}$), F1-Score ($2 \times \frac{\text{Precision} \times \text{Recall}}{\text{Precision} + \text{Recall}}$), and Area Under the ROC Curve (AUC).
  * *Segmentation Metrics:* Dice Similarity Coefficient ($\text{Dice} = \frac{2|X \cap Y|}{|X| + |Y|}$) and Intersection over Union / Jaccard Index ($\text{IoU} = \frac{|X \cap Y|}{|X \cup Y|}$).

* How do you usually collect your data or samples?
The authors constructed the **CKHK-22** dataset (comprising 14,287 annotated images) by aggregating and curating three prominent public colonoscopy databases: CVC-ClinicDB (clinical polyp frames), Kvasir-2 (gastrointestinal conditions including ulcerative colitis and esophagitis), and Hyper-Kvasir (broad upper and lower GI tract pathologies). The initial 24 imbalanced classes were consolidated and filtered into 10 well-balanced classes (polyps, hemorrhoids, cecum, z-line, pylorus, dyed resection margins, etc.). Preprocessing included median filtering to remove salt-and-pepper noise, pixel intensity normalization to [0, 1], resizing to $224 \times 224$, scheduled data augmentation ($\pm 15^\circ$ rotation, horizontal/vertical flips, $\pm 10\%$ scaling, $\pm 20\%$ luminance shifting), and stratified 80:20 train-test splitting to prevent data leakage.

## 3. Obstacles & Breakthroughs
* What is the biggest challenge you've faced with this project?
Overcoming severe class imbalance between critical diagnostic categories (e.g., 2,003 hemorrhoid images vs. 818 polyp images) while handling high intra-class morphological variability, subtle lesion boundaries, salt-and-pepper endoscopic noise, and the substantial computational/memory overhead inherent to transformer models processing high-dimensional medical imagery.

* Have you hit any dead ends or unexpected results yet?
Initial training on the raw 24-class dataset led to poor generalization and inconsistent performance on under-represented classes, necessitating an architectural and taxonomic reduction to 10 balanced classes. Furthermore, standard CNNs (VGG-16 and ResNet-50) struggled to capture long-range contextual relationships, while conventional segmentation baselines (U-Net and SegNet) achieved inadequate boundary demarcation (Dice 0.75–0.84, IoU < 0.68). In classification testing, despite ViT's high overall accuracy (97%), the model produced 100 false negatives (misclassifying polyps as hemorrhoids, resulting in a polyp recall of 0.86 vs. 1.00 for hemorrhoids), underscoring the ongoing challenge of eliminating missed malignant lesions.

* What has been your favorite finding or "eureka" moment?
The synergistic coupling of Vision Transformers with DeepLabV3+: ViT's multi-head self-attention mechanism captured global visual relationships to achieve 99.98% training accuracy, 97.0% testing accuracy, 98% precision, and 92% AUC, while DeepLabV3+ with ASPP delivered state-of-the-art lesion boundary segmentation with a 0.88 Dice Coefficient and 0.71 IoU. This dual-pipeline surpassed classical CNNs and heavy multi-model ensembles while remaining lightweight enough for real-time inference on a single workstation GPU (NVIDIA Quadro P5000) and cloud environments.

## 4. Lit Review & The "Gap"
* What major gap did you find in the previous research?
Most prior literature treated classification and segmentation as separate, isolated tasks rather than integrating them into a cohesive clinical CADx pipeline. Existing studies predominantly trained on small, single-center datasets (such as isolated CVC-ClinicDB or Kvasir), limiting demographic generalizability. Methodologically, standard CNNs failed to capture long-range spatial dependencies, while real-time segmentation approaches (e.g., ColonSegNet) sacrificed boundary precision for speed, and classical segmentors (U-Net, SegNet) lacked multi-scale contextual awareness for irregular lesions.

* Who is the go-to author or foundation paper in your field?
Key foundation works and benchmark authors include:
* *Benchmark Gastrointestinal Datasets:* Bernal et al. (CVC-ClinicDB, 2015), Pogorelov et al. (Kvasir, 2017), and Borgli et al. (Hyper-Kvasir, 2020).
* *Convolutional Backbones:* He et al. (ResNet-50, 2016), Huang et al. (DenseNet-201, 2017), and Simonyan & Zisserman (VGG-16, 2014).
* *Vision Transformers & Attention:* Dosovitskiy et al. (Vision Transformers / ViT, 2020) and Vaswani et al. (Attention Is All You Need, 2017).
* *Semantic Segmentation:* Chen et al. (DeepLabV3+, 2018).
* *Preceding CADx Ensembles by the Research Group:* GIEnsemformerCADx (2024) and EnsemDeepCADx (2024).

* Did you find any major contradictions between previous studies?
A major trade-off and contradiction in prior literature exists between diagnostic precision and computational deployability: complex multi-tier ensemble systems (e.g., AlexNet + DenseNet-201 + Transformer + SVM) achieve high testing accuracy (99.0%) but require prohibitive multi-GPU resources and long training times that prevent practical real-time clinical adoption. Conversely, lightweight networks often fail on fine-grained lesion boundaries. The authors resolve this contradiction by demonstrating that PCA-filtered ViT combined with ASPP-equipped DeepLabV3+ achieves near-ensemble accuracy (97.0%) and high segmentation fidelity (0.88 Dice) with moderate hardware demands and real-time cloud feasibility.

## 5. Future Impact & Advice
* What do you think is the next step for this research area?
Integrating explainability frameworks (such as Grad-CAM or LIME) into the clinical user interface to generate visual attribution heatmaps for clinician verification; implementing feedback-driven continual learning where the CADx system continuously adapts to newly observed clinical data; expanding the dataset to encompass rare colorectal variants; and deploying the pipeline onto live video feeds during colonoscopy procedures with voice-enabled diagnostic guidance.

* Where do you see your findings being applied in real life?
Real-time automated decision-support software deployed in endoscopy suites and operating rooms to assist gastroenterologists during colonoscopies, as well as cloud-hosted tele-screening platforms (via Google Cloud) that provide expert-level diagnostic screening in rural, community, and resource-limited healthcare facilities.

* Any tips for someone trying to read papers in this domain?
Focus on how modern hybrid architectures reconcile local spatial feature extraction (via CNNs and atrous convolutions) with global contextual modeling (via Vision Transformers). Carefully evaluate data curation practices—specifically checking how authors handle extreme class imbalances, cross-dataset domain shifts, and data leakage during augmentation—and prioritize clinical utility metrics (Dice coefficient, IoU, Sensitivity/Recall, and inference latency) over isolated top-line accuracy.
