## Editorial: AI in digital oncology: imaging and wearable technology for cancer detection and management

## Introduction

Cancer remains the second leading cause of death in the United States, with persistent disparities affecting minority groups and individuals with limited healthcare access. While mortality rates have declined for many cancer types, others like pancreatic and thyroid cancers continue to show stagnant or increasing rates. This Research Topic explores how digital health tools, artificial intelligence (AI), machine learning (ML), and computational techniques can address challenges in early cancer detection, diagnosis, treatment planning, and monitoring—with particular focus on reducing healthcare disparities through technological advancement.

## Key contributions and clinical innovations

The papers in this Research Topic demonstrate remarkable progress in applying digital health and AI to cancer care, offering novel solutions to longstanding clinical challenges.

## Addressing digital health disparities

Pana et al.'s study of 270 digital health technology (DHT) users in Romania revealed that DHT users had 2–6 times higher cancer screening rates than the general population, with uptake concentrated among internet-literate subgroups. This highlights both the promise and peril of digital health innovations—while empowering proactive disease management, they risk widening gaps for digitally marginalized populations. The authors advocate systematic integration of DHT tools into primary care workflows to bridge educational and logistical barriers.

## AI applications in gastrointestinal cancers

Akbari et al. provide a comprehensive review of AI applications across esophageal, gastric, hepatocellular, colorectal, and pancreatic cancers. Their analysis reveals ML models achieved on average 89% accuracy in analyzing endoscopic and CT images, with convolutional neural networks demonstrating particular strength in identifying polyps during colonoscopy. However, they emphasize that successful clinical integration requires addressing data quality issues, interpretability concerns, and standardization needs through multidisciplinary collaboration.

## Precision immunotherapy using AI

Wang et al. developed a Transformer-Unet deep learning network to predict PD-L1 expression from routine H&E-stained lung cancer images, eliminating the need for costly immunohistochemical staining. Their model achieved 80% dice similarity coefficient and 0.92 correlation with gold standard assessment—exceeding consistency between different pathologists. This breakthrough could democratize access to precision immunotherapy by making biomarker assessment available in resource-limited settings.

## AI performance in hepatocellular carcinoma (HCC)

Al-Obeidat et al.'s meta-analysis of seven studies comparing AI models with physicians in HCC detection found comparable sensitivity (93%) but highlighted complementary strengths. Region-based convolutional neural networks showed particularly high sensitivity (96%), while physicians maintained superior specificity (100%). This reinforces AI's role as an effective “second reader” rather than replacement for human expertise.

## Computational tumor microenvironment analysis

Khanduri et al. used multiplex immunofluorescence and spatial analytics to map macrophage subtypes in colorectal cancer liver metastases. They discovered that M2 macrophages predominated at tumor periphery with greater proximity to malignant cells, while higher M1 macrophage densities in tumor centers paradoxically correlated with poorer survival—challenging traditional immunology paradigms and demonstrating how computational approaches can reveal prognostic biomarkers invisible to conventional assessment.

## The next frontier: generative AI and multi-agent systems

The evolution toward generative AI and multi-agent systems represents a paradigm shift from isolated AI applications toward comprehensive, coordinated intelligence spanning the entire cancer care ecosystem.

## Generative AI applications

Large language models can synthesize vast medical literature, generate personalized patient explanations, and create adaptive interfaces accommodating varying health literacy levels. For populations with limited digital access—a concern highlighted in Pana et al.'s work—generative AI could provide conversational interfaces requiring minimal technical sophistication while delivering personalized cancer risk information.

## Multi-agent cancer care workflows

Unlike monolithic AI models, multi-agent systems comprise specialized components collaborating through structured workflows. A comprehensive cancer care system might include:

* Screening agents analyzing routine imaging and EHR data for elevated cancer risk
* Diagnostic agents specialized in different modalities (radiology, pathology, genomics)
* Treatment planning agents incorporating molecular profiling and trial evidence
* Monitoring agents tracking treatment response and surveillance
* Patient communication agents generating personalized educational materials

This distributed architecture offers several advantages: modularity allowing individual agent optimization, transparency through decomposed reasoning steps, robustness through diverse modeling approaches, and adaptive resource allocation for critical tasks.

For example, Wang et al.'s PD-L1 prediction work could be handled by a dedicated histopathology agent while other agents focus on radiologic or genomic aspects, with integration agents synthesizing findings into comprehensive treatment recommendations.

## AI literacy across the care continuum

Successful AI integration requires cultivating AI literacy—understanding AI capabilities, limitations, and appropriate applications—across all stakeholders.

* **Healthcare Provider Literacy** encompasses critical evaluation of AI outputs, appropriate patient communication about AI-derived insights, and awareness of potential biases. Training programs must integrate AI literacy into medical education at all levels, emphasizing complementary human-AI collaboration rather than algorithmic deference.
* **Patient and Caregiver Literacy** focuses on understanding AI's role in care decisions, appropriate trust calibration, and privacy implications. Educational materials should use accessible language that empowers rather than mystifies, particularly important given digital literacy's influence on healthcare engagement demonstrated in Pana et al.'s findings.
* **Health System Literacy** requires governance frameworks for responsible deployment, quality assurance processes, and health equity impact assessments to prevent algorithmic biases from perpetuating cancer care disparities.

## Implementation challenges and future directions

Despite promising results, significant challenges remain in translating AI from research to clinical practice:

## Data quality and standardization

AI performance depends heavily on training data quality and representativeness. Standardizing collection and processing protocols across institutions remains a critical hurdle, particularly for diverse histological image data and physician interpretation variability. Such standardization approaches need contextual consideration and appropriate refinement for subsequent data-derived models and their associated inferences/predictions as well. Concepts like “model-scorecards” (Cherian et al., 2024), data ontologies and conformal inference approaches (Mitchell et al., 2019) might be relevant to bring into our collective knowledge-base in this regard.

## Interpretability and trust

Many AI models function as “black boxes,” challenging clinical acceptance. Multi-agent architectures offer promising solutions by decomposing complex reasoning into transparent, discrete steps performed by specialized components.

## Population diversity

Most AI models are trained on specific demographic groups, raising generalizability concerns. Future work must prioritize diverse data inclusion and explicit performance assessment across demographic groups to ensure equitable benefit rather than exacerbated disparities.

## Workflow integration

Successful implementation requires seamless integration with existing clinical workflows through user-friendly interfaces, clear uncertainty communication, and decision support systems augmenting rather than replacing human expertise.

## Reducing disparities through technology

DHTs and AI technologies offer multiple pathways to extend high-quality cancer care to underserved populations: automating labor-intensive diagnostic tasks (reducing specialized expertise requirements), enabling remote assessment and triage, standardizing care quality regardless of provider experience, and supporting earlier detection across all populations.

However, realizing these benefits requires thoughtful implementation considering access, affordability, and cultural appropriateness—ensuring DHTs and AI reduce rather than widen existing healthcare gaps.

## Conclusion: a vision for equitable AI-enhanced cancer care

This Research Topic illustrates DHT and AI's transformative potential across cancer detection and monitoring pipelines. The emergence of generative AI and multi-agent systems promises evolution from isolated applications toward comprehensive, coordinated intelligence spanning the entire cancer care ecosystem.

Looking forward, we envision seamlessly integrated DHT and AI technologies supporting early detection, accurate diagnosis, personalized treatment planning, and effective monitoring while augmenting rather than replacing human expertise. Multi-agent workflows offer particularly promising approaches for orchestrating complex cancer care pathways with maintained transparency and interpretability.

Critically, this vision includes equitable access to DHT and AI-enhanced cancer care for all populations, regardless of geographic location, socioeconomic status, or demographic characteristics. By thoughtfully developing and implementing these technologies with attention to disparities, we can advance both technical capabilities and meaningful progress toward health equity.

The research presented here represents significant steps toward this vision. Through continued collaboration between computer scientists, oncologists, and healthcare professionals, we can create a future where DHTs and AI help reduce cancer burden for all patients. The frontier of early cancer detection is here—the challenge now is shaping it wisely, inclusively, and with unwavering commitment to improving patient lives while ensuring digital and AI literacy becomes as fundamental to cancer care as the technologies themselves.


---

## Artificial intelligence in inflammatory bowel disease

## Abstract

Inflammatory bowel disease (IBD) is a complex condition influenced by various intestinal factors. Advances in next-generation sequencing, high-throughput omics, and molecular network technologies have significantly accelerated research in this field. The emergence of artificial intelligence (AI) has further enhanced the efficient utilization and interpretation of datasets, enabling the discovery of clinically actionable insights. AI is now extensively applied in gastroenterology, where it aids in endoscopic analyses, including the diagnosis of colorectal cancer, precancerous polyps, gastrointestinal inflammatory lesions, and bleeding. Additionally, AI supports clinicians in patient stratification, predicting disease progression and treatment responses, and adjusting treatment plans in a timely manner. This approach not only reduces healthcare costs but also improves patient health and safety. This review outlines the principles of AI, the current research landscape, and future directions for its applications in IBD, with the goal of advancing targeted treatment strategies.

## INTRODUCTION

Inflammatory bowel disease (IBD) is an immune-driven condition primarily comprising ulcerative colitis (UC) and Crohn’s disease (CD). Although IBD affects approximately 1% of the global population, its incidence is increasing worldwide, with the highest rates observed in Europe and North America and a notable rise in Asia as well [1]. The precise cause of IBD remains elusive, but it is generally believed to result from a complex interaction of genetic, microbial, environmental, and immune factors [2]. The management, diagnosis, and treatment decision-making processes for IBD are intricate, involving the exploration of novel diagnostic methods, identification of new biomarkers, monitoring drug efficacy, assessing prognosis, and developing new therapeutic strategies. These areas remain central to research and present significant challenges in the field of IBD.

In recent years, endoscopy has emerged as a key tool in the diagnosis, treatment, and monitoring of IBD. Techniques such as dye chromoendoscopy (DCE) and virtual chromoendoscopy (VCE) are commonly employed in IBD endoscopy. However, despite the utility of these methods, variations in specific application techniques, the quality of diagnostic components, and the interpretation of results can significantly impact the effectiveness of diagnosis and treatment by gastroenterologists. Artificial intelligence (AI) shows great promise in addressing these challenges, enhancing diagnostic accuracy, and ultimately reducing both the duration of the disease and the associated medical costs.

In the context of IBD, AI systems can simulate complex measurements and judgments, enabling precise disease assessment with reduced bias and offering valuable insights into the disease’s pathophysiology. AI facilitates more efficient data analysis, enabling a deeper understanding of the role specific genes play in IBD risk and progression [3]. Furthermore, AI has proven highly effective in identifying active disease, calculating disease activity scores, predicting recurrence risk, and supporting real-time treatment decisions [4,5]. This article will explore AI’s role in the pathogenesis, diagnosis, symptom assessment, prognosis, drug response, IBD-related colorectal cancer (CRC), and the development of new treatments for IBD, with the aim of improving comprehensive diagnosis and treatment strategies for the condition.

## DEFINITION OF ARTIFICIAL INTELLIGENCE

AI replicates human intelligence using data-driven algorithms that are guided and refined through experience and data analysis, a process commonly known as machine learning (ML) [6]. These models can integrate extensive qualitative and quantitative data derived from medical images and clinical information, helping physicians make decisions more efficiently [7]. In supervised learning, algorithms are trained on labeled datasets to identify patterns linked to particular groups, such as healthy versus sick individuals. Commonly used supervised ML models are random forests (RFs), neural networks (NNs), and support vector machines (SVMs) [8]. Unsupervised models utilize datasets without labels, allowing algorithms to identify patterns in the data based on similar features without any prior knowledge of specific diagnoses or outcomes [9].

## APPLICATION OF ARTIFICIAL INTELLIGENCE IN IBD

### Pathogenesis
The pathogenesis of IBD remains incompletely understood, though it is widely accepted that a variety of factors—such as genomics, proteomics, metabolomics, and lifestyle—contribute to its development. Recent advancements in ML and other analytical methods have significantly deepened our understanding of the transcriptomic, proteomic, and metabolomic landscapes of IBD. Notably, research integrating metagenomics, meta-proteomics, and hierarchical clustering analysis has made important strides. For instance, Erickson et al. [10] identified a reduction in certain microbial proteins, including Firmicutes proteins associated with the butyrate pathway, in CD patients with ileal lesions. Additionally, transcriptomic analysis of human ileal and colon samples has provided insights into the complex inflammatory pathways that drive IBD pathology. An ML model developed by Isakov et al. [11], leveraging genome-wide association study (GWAS) data, pinpointed over 240 genes potentially involved in the etiology of IBD [12]. However, the genetic diversity inherent in genomic variants poses a considerable challenge when analyzing the extensive and complex genomic data using conventional analytical tools. Studies have shown that ML algorithms can mitigate some of these limitations by enhancing the analysis of GWAS data, particularly through techniques such as incremental feature selection [13].

### Diagnosis
The management of IBD involves three distinct phases: diagnosis, disease assessment and treatment selection, and follow-up evaluation with potential treatment adjustments [14]. IBD classification primarily relies on endoscopic examination and tissue biopsies. However, these methods often fail to fully capture the disease’s spectrum or to adequately address the specific presentations seen in patients with extraintestinal manifestations.

#### Endoscope
Numerous studies have demonstrated that deep neural networks (DNNs) can assess intestinal mucosal inflammation and its resolution using only endoscopic images, thereby reducing the reliance on pathology. While these systems exhibit high objectivity and accuracy, there is still a lack of large-scale clinical data to validate their effectiveness [15,16]. Bossuyt et al. [17] developed an objective endoscopy scoring system that employs pixel color analysis, vascular pattern recognition, and other parameters, operating independently of the clinician. Additionally, other models that use confocal laser microendoscopy have shown the ability to deliver IBD diagnoses with high sensitivity and specificity [18]. VCE is a key method for detecting suspected or confirmed small intestinal CD [19]. However, the review of VCE is time-consuming and labor-intensive as it involves examining thousands of frames. The ML model studied by Brodersen et al. [20] can not only shorten the diagnosis time of clinicians but also improve the overall accuracy. Barash et al. [21] successfully developed a CNN model to assist in capsule endoscopy, enabling automatic grading of CD ulcers and facilitating CD diagnosis and monitoring [22].

#### Imaging diagnosis
Computer-aided scoring can help to reduce the differences caused by individual subjectivity in the evaluation of CT/MRI images of IBD. The accuracy of the radiomics model based on ML constructed by domestic scholars [23] in predicting the presence or absence of intestinal fibrosis in IBD (AUC = 0.816) was significantly higher than that of radiologists (AUC = 0.556). Shi et al. [24] developed an ML classification system by combining six selected radiomics features with eight classification algorithms. The performance of semiautomated image analysis software in assessing structural intestinal injury related to CD in abdominal CT enterography data was comparable to that of experienced radiologists (the accuracy was 0.876) [25]. Furthermore, ML methods have been utilized to predict CD severity grades from abdominal MRI data [26].

#### Pathological diagnosis
AI in pathology enables precise diagnosis and better stratification of IBD. A CNN model trained on the entire slice image can effectively assist pathologists by filtering out nondiagnostic slices while maintaining optimal sensitivity [28]. Vande Casteele et al. [29] digitized colonic biopsy samples from UC patients, and the model trained on this data found that eosinophil density was associated with disease severity. Furlanello et al. [30] developed a DL model for plasma cell segmentation. AI has also made significant progress in predicting IBD dysplasia. A recently developed AI system was used to predict p53 immunohistochemical staining on H and E-stained slides, achieving a sensitivity, specificity, and accuracy ranging from 0.73 to 0.83, 0.91 to 0.92, and 0.86 to 0.91, respectively [31].

#### Biomarker
Studies have shown that the pathogenesis of IBD is linked to the intestinal microbiome. ML algorithms utilizing microbiome data can assist clinicians in diagnosing IBD more effectively [32,33]. Gevers et al. [34] successfully distinguished patients with CD from those with other gastrointestinal disorders by combining rectal mucosa-associated microbiome signatures with clinical data. ML models using proteomics data can assist clinicians in better categorizing IBD patients [35,36,37]. Some researchers have used ML models (such as RF and SVM) to demonstrate that circulating miRNA signatures in blood could serve as diagnostic markers for IBD [38,39].

### Assessment of disease
Predicting disease progression is crucial for developing personalized treatment strategies for IBD. AI can identify subtle visual features during endoscopy and make histological inferences without the need for biopsies. CNN-based ML algorithms have shown promise in assessing UC and CD severity via endoscopy [43,44]. Gui et al. [45,46] created a CNN-based system for histological diagnosis and scoring of UC. Deep learning models using Geboes scoring systems can effectively distinguish the microscopic disease activity of IBD [47]. The specialized computer-aided detection (CADe) system based on CNN has effectively identified the remission or activity state of the mucosa [48,49]. Additionally, Reddy et al. [50] achieved an impressive accuracy (AUC = 0.93) in predicting CD severity using an ML model. Similarly, an RF model developed by Waljee et al. [51] successfully predicted inpatient admissions in IBD with an AUC of 0.87. Lee et al. [52] developed models using genome-wide association studies to identify four loci linked to CD prognosis. Takenaka et al. [53] developed a DNN prediction model that accurately forecasts endoscopic mucosal healing as well as the risks of hospitalization and colectomy.

### Drug reaction
In order to address poor drug response, increasing studies focus on using ML methods to predict drug response and analyze mechanisms of loss of response in IBD treatment [55]. Popa et al. [56] developed an AI algorithm that utilizes clinical and endoscopic data to accurately distinguish UC patients who are likely to achieve clinical remission from those with active disease. Furthermore, RF models leveraging clinical data from trials have also shown strong performance in predicting CD and UC patient responses to biological agents like infliximab, vedolizumab, and ustekinumab [57,58]. Waljee et al. [59,60,61] used data from vedolizumab clinical trials to develop RF ML models. The RF model developed by Shaw et al. [62] analyzed longitudinal microbiome data from pediatric IBD patients treated with biologics. Kazemian et al. [63] demonstrated that FMT outcomes depend on microbiome structure. Jones et al. [64] analyzed the gut microbiome in fecal samples. Recently, Iacucc et al. [65] found that computer-aided imaging analysis of probe-based confocal laser microendoscopy, combined with molecular markers and gene expression, can effectively identify response markers for biological therapy in IBD patients.

### Application of AI in IBD-related CRC
ML algorithms can serve as noninvasive and cost-effective methods for screening large populations at risk for CRC by utilizing personal health data [68]. The AI-driven CADe system enhances real-time examination quality by addressing issues like exit time and blind spots, potentially improving adenoma detection rates in IBD patients [70,71]. Mori et al. [72] demonstrated that CADx assistance helps endoscopists differentiate between neoplastic and non-neoplastic polyps. AI models can support endoscopists in the learning phase, help distinguish between tumor and nontumor lesions, and predict submucosal invasion depth in CRC patients, acting as virtual assistants for physicians [73].

### New therapeutic drugs
In the realm of IBD, AI has been successfully applied to facilitate new drug research and development [74]. Nuzzo et al. [75] used computational methods to identify nearly a thousand metabolite–human target interactions from IBD public databases. Sahoo et al. [77] utilized ML methods to construct a network of gene clusters that preserve intestinal epithelial barrier integrity, paving the way for the development of agents aimed at protecting the intestinal barrier in IBD.

## CHALLENGES IN THE CLINICAL APPLICATION OF AI IN IBD

Despite the promising applications of AI, several challenges remain:

1. Due to the “black box” nature of AI, it is difficult to identify, correct, and prevent issues such as algorithmic defects, training data biases, or model overfitting in clinical settings.
2. AI algorithms require high-quality data for training and validation to make accurate predictions. However, current sample sizes and large-scale prospective data in AI research for IBD are insufficient, raising concerns about the applicability of these AI models in clinical practice.
3. AI applications in clinical settings raise potential ethical and legal concerns, especially regarding data privacy and security.

## CONCLUSION

As an emerging field, AI is advancing rapidly but remains in its early stages. This review highlights AI’s role in diagnosing, monitoring, and prognosis of IBD. To enhance disease progression and severity predictions, future AI models must integrate more comprehensive and longitudinal data, while expanding data resources, mining health records, and combining them with multiomics information. Additionally, strengthening collaboration between medical and AI researchers is essential to accelerate AI integration into clinical practice.

## Conflicts of interest
There are no conflicts of interest.

## Artificial intelligence-based multimodal model for the identification of ulcerative colitis with concomitant cytomegalovirus colitis (DOI: 10.1177/17562848251364194)
**Core ML Technology:** Multimodal Dynamic Affine Transformation network (DAFTNet) combining a ResNet convolutional neural network backbone with Squeeze-and-Excitation (SE) channel attention modules and tabular clinical biomarker integration. The dynamic affine transformation (G = B * F + E) modulates convolutional feature maps using channel weights (F) and bias offsets (E) derived from concatenating pooled image features (D) and 8 laboratory biomarkers (A: WBC, neutrophils, hemoglobin, eosinophils, lymphocytes, platelets, ESR, IgA). Trained with Cross-Entropy Loss, Adam optimizer (learning rate = 1e-4), gradient clipping, batch size 10, 200 epochs on an NVIDIA GeForce RTX 3060 (12 GB VRAM), with OpenCV contour-based preprocessing.

## 1. The "Big Picture" & Motivation
* What is the main problem your research is trying to solve?
Distinguishing ulcerative colitis (UC) flare-ups from concomitant cytomegalovirus (CMV) colitis during colonoscopy. Both conditions present with overlapping clinical symptoms (hematochezia, diarrhea, fever) and endoscopic appearances, while standard pathological confirmation methods (HE staining, IHC, CMV-DNA in situ hybridization) suffer from variable sensitivity, observer dependence, procedural delays, and biopsy sampling errors.

* What made you choose this specific topic?
The incidence of UC is rapidly increasing, and immunosuppressive therapy (steroids, biologics, immunomodulators) places patients at a 3- to 5-fold higher risk of opportunistic CMV infection. Concomitant CMV infection exacerbates disease activity, induces steroid resistance, and significantly increases colectomy and mortality risks, making early recognition crucial to guide timely antiviral therapy and avoid unnecessary surgery.

* Why does this research matter for the industry right now?
Providing real-time diagnostic decision support during endoscopy allows clinicians to pinpoint high-risk lesions for targeted biopsy and rapidly initiate antiviral treatment. By integrating routine, low-cost laboratory biomarkers with computer vision, this approach improves diagnostic precision and cost-effectiveness over standalone image models without adding clinical burden.

## 2. The Practical Approach (Methodology)
* Are you doing mostly lab experiments, data analysis, or fieldwork?
This research is a retrospective clinical data analysis and diagnostic artificial intelligence study using electronic health records and colonoscopy image datasets from Peking Union Medical College Hospital (2015–2023).

* What framework or formulas are you using to analyze things?
The study employs a deep learning multimodal Dynamic Affine Transformation (DAFT) framework with a ResNet backbone and Squeeze-and-Excitation (SE) channel attention. The core transformation formula is G = B * F + E, where feature maps B from layer 3 are globally pooled via `AdaptiveAvgPool2d` to vector D, concatenated with clinical biomarker vector A, and transformed via fully connected layers into bias vector E and channel weight vector F. The model is trained using the cross-entropy loss function, Adam optimizer (learning rate = 1e-4), gradient clipping, and data augmentation (random flips and rotations). Performance is evaluated using confusion matrix metrics (Accuracy, Sensitivity, Specificity, PPV, NPV) and ROC-AUC curves following STARD guidelines. Image cropping is executed via OpenCV's `findContours`.

* How do you usually collect your data or samples?
Data was collected retrospectively from 174 moderate-to-severe UC patients (87 with pathologically verified CMV colitis via HE inclusion bodies, positive IHC, or CMV-DNA in situ hybridization; and 87 age- and Mayo-score-matched UC controls). A total of 3,345 colonoscopy images (1,115 CMV colitis, 1,115 UC without CMV, 1,115 normal) were preprocessed to remove extraneous text/borders via OpenCV contour extraction, randomly partitioned into training (70%, 2,340 samples) and testing (30%) sets, and paired with 8 routine laboratory biomarkers (WBC, neutrophil count, hemoglobin, eosinophil count, lymphocyte count, platelet count, ESR, and IgA).

## 3. Obstacles & Breakthroughs
* What is the biggest challenge you've faced with this project?
The pronounced overlap between the endoscopic appearances and clinical symptoms of severe UC exacerbations and CMV colitis, alongside the inherent performance limitations of unimodal vision models that yield low sensitivity and high false-positive rates when unassisted by clinical context.

* Have you hit any dead ends or unexpected results yet?
Purely image-based deep learning models (ResNet and SeNet) encountered a performance bottleneck, showing relatively low sensitivity and positive predictive values for identifying CMV colitis. Additionally, static still images lack full colon spatial distribution context and remain susceptible to real-world quality degradation (such as blurred views from poor bowel preparation).

* What has been your favorite finding or "eureka" moment?
The realization that dynamic multimodal fusion (DAFTNet) produced a substantial performance leap—achieving 0.91 accuracy, 0.869 sensitivity, 0.931 specificity, and 0.95 ROC-AUC for CMV colitis, outperforming attention-based SeNet by 6.5% in accuracy and 7.1% in specificity. Feature importance analysis revealed that non-imaging clinical biomarkers (especially platelet count, hemoglobin, ESR, and eosinopenia) provide independent, complementary signals that directly modulate visual convolutional feature maps to reduce false positives.

## 4. Lit Review & The "Gap"
* What major gap did you find in the previous research?
Previous machine learning and deep learning studies in gastroenterology were largely restricted to unimodal data (either purely endoscopic imaging or purely tabular omics/laboratory biomarkers) or relied on complex model ensembling over tiny sample sizes (e.g., Kim et al., n = 34, 86 images) which hindered interpretability and clinical scalability. There was a lack of end-to-end multimodal frameworks capable of bidirectional dynamic information exchange between routine clinical biomarkers and endoscopic imaging for UC/CMV differentiation.

* Who is the go-to author or foundation paper in your field?
Key foundation papers and authors include:
- Pölsterl et al. (2021) for the Dynamic Affine Feature Map Transform (DAFT) multimodal architecture.
- Yang et al. for defining characteristic endoscopic features of CMV colitis in UC (e.g., punched-out ulcerations, cobblestone appearance).
- Guimaraes et al. for convolutional neural networks and hybrid algorithms differentiating IBD, infectious colitis, and ischemic colitis.
- Kim et al. for early deep learning ensemble approaches in CMV colitis detection.
- He et al. (ResNet) and Hu et al. (Squeeze-and-Excitation Networks) for foundational computer vision backbones.

* Did you find any major contradictions between previous studies?
While prior literature suggested that endoscopic visual features alone might suffice for AI classification, unimodal deep learning models in practice suffered from low sensitivity and required heavy ensembling to achieve acceptable results. Furthermore, earlier hybrid algorithms combining clinical and imaging features showed poor sensitivity for IBD and low specificity for infectious colitis, whereas the DAFT dynamic affine modulation successfully resolved this by enabling clinical biomarkers to quantitatively scale and bias visual feature maps.

## 5. Future Impact & Advice
* What do you think is the next step for this research area?
Conducting prospective multicenter external validation across diverse patient cohorts and endoscopic imaging hardware; expanding models from still images to real-time video-based architectures capable of analyzing continuous colonoscopy video streams; and evaluating model performance against other confounding infectious colitides such as Epstein-Barr virus (EBV) colitis.

* Where do you see your findings being applied in real life?
Embedded into real-time computer-aided diagnosis (CADe/CADx) software within endoscopy suites to highlight suspicious CMV colitis lesions during live colonoscopy, prompting targeted biopsies and accelerating antiviral therapeutic decisions.

* Any tips for someone trying to read papers in this domain?
Pay close attention to how ground truth pathological diagnoses are defined (e.g., HE staining vs. IHC/in situ hybridization), evaluate whether deep learning models are unimodal or multimodal, examine sensitivity and specificity trade-offs specifically in pre-biopsy screening contexts, and scrutinize how feature fusion mechanisms (such as dynamic affine transformations) provide clinical interpretability beyond black-box predictions.

## Digital biomarkers and artificial intelligence: a new frontier in personalized management of inflammatory bowel disease

## Abstract
Artificial intelligence (AI) is rapidly gaining traction in gastroenterology, particularly in the management of inflammatory bowel disease (IBD). Given the complexity of IBD care, AI offers the potential to enhance diagnosis, monitoring, and treatment. This review aims to summarize recent developments in AI applications for IBD and identify key challenges and opportunities for future research and clinical implementation. AI has demonstrated utility in multiple domains of IBD management, including endoscopic disease activity assessment, histological evaluation, imaging interpretation, prediction of disease course, treatment response, and real-world data integration. Despite promising accuracy and utility, most models remain in early development stages and lack widespread clinical validation. Major barriers include data heterogeneity, limited generalizability, and regulatory uncertainties. AI has significant potential to revolutionize IBD care. Continued multidisciplinary collaboration, validation in diverse clinical settings, and integration into clinical workflows are critical for realizing its full impact.

## Introduction
Inflammatory Bowel Disease (IBD), encompassing Crohn’s disease (CD) and ulcerative colitis (UC), is a chronic, immune-mediated and relapsing condition that significantly impacts patients' quality of life. Diagnosis relies primarily on colonoscopy with terminal ileum intubation. Despite advances in therapeutics, accurate disease monitoring and personalized treatment strategies remain major challenges. Traditional biomarkers (CRP, FC) and endoscopy are essential but limited by episodic, invasive, and costly nature.

The evolution of digital health—wearable sensors, smartphone apps, remote monitoring—combined with AI and machine learning (ML), promises to revolutionize IBD management by enabling continuous, real-time monitoring, early flare detection, and personalized therapeutic adjustments.

## Digital biomarkers
Digital biomarkers are objective, quantifiable physiological and behavioral data collected via digital devices. Unlike conventional biomarkers providing episodic data, digital biomarkers offer dynamic, continuous views of disease activity. Studies (e.g., Hirten et al.) show wearable metrics (HR, HRV, steps) can identify subclinical inflammation and predict flares weeks in advance. However, continuous monitoring might increase patient anxiety.

## Correlation with standard biomarkers
Emerging evidence shows strong correlations between digital biomarkers and traditional indicators. For example, wearable sensors measuring CRP, IL-6, and calprotectin in sweat correlate well with serum assays.

## The role of AI in IBD: unlocking hidden patterns
AI (encompassing ML and deep learning) can perform complex tasks, including pattern recognition and predictive modeling, to advance precision medicine. Combining Digital Health (DH) and AI enables proactive, individualized IBD care, shifting away from standard-of-care (SoC) episodic visits toward more efficient management.

## Machine learning and predictive analytics
ML models (supervised/unsupervised/deep learning) decode patterns in large datasets, predicting disease trajectories, treatment responses, and identifying novel therapeutic targets. Recent studies demonstrate AI's utility in histology (e.g., Nancy Histological Index analysis) and predicting clinical remission.

## The expanding role of AI in IBD detection and classification
AI/ML, particularly CNNs, enhances diagnostic accuracy in distinguishing CD from UC using molecular, omics, and imaging data. While AI offers standardization, experienced clinicians remain superior in complex diagnostic scenarios, suggesting AI should complement—not replace—expert judgment.

## AI-driven assessment in IBD
AI tools for assessing disease activity (biochemical, endoscopic, histological) provide increased precision and reproducibility. CNNs automate endoscopic scoring and histological remission classification with high consistency.

## AI-Based prediction of treatment response
Precision medicine enabled by AI allows prediction of therapeutic response to biologics, surgical risk, and prognosis by integrating multi-omics, demographic, and clinical data.

## Remote monitoring and predictive analytics
Digital health tools (wearables, mHealth apps, telemedicine) facilitate continuous, real-time monitoring of physiological and patient-reported outcomes, shifting care toward a proactive model.

## Challenges and future directions
Implementing AI and digital biomarkers in IBD requires addressing several barriers:
* **Adherence:** Long-term digital engagement can decline due to fatigue.
* **Privacy/Ethics:** Data security, ownership, and transparent secondary use policies are critical, especially given the sensitivity of IBD data.
* **Regulatory:** Current frameworks (GDPR, HIPAA) are insufficient for real-time, adaptive AI. Global harmonization is needed.
* **Clinical Integration:** Further validation and seamless integration into workflows are essential.

## Systematic review of cost effectiveness and budget impact of artificial intelligence in healthcare

## Abstract
This systematic review examines the cost-effectiveness, utility, and budget impact of clinical artificial intelligence (AI) interventions across diverse healthcare settings. Nineteen studies spanning oncology, cardiology, ophthalmology, and infectious diseases demonstrate that AI improves diagnostic accuracy, enhances quality-adjusted life years, and reduces costs—largely by minimizing unnecessary procedures and optimizing resource use. Several interventions achieved incremental cost-effectiveness ratios well below accepted thresholds. However, many evaluations relied on static models that may overestimate benefits by not capturing the adaptive learning of AI systems over time. Additionally, indirect costs, infrastructure investments, and equity considerations were often underreported, suggesting that reported economic benefits may be overstated. Dynamic modeling indicates sustained long-term value, but further research is needed to incorporate comprehensive cost components and subgroup analyses. These findings underscore the clinical promise and economic complexity of AI in healthcare, emphasizing the need for context-specific, methodologically robust evaluations to guide future policy and practice effectively.

## 1. Introduction
The rapid evolution of AI in medicine is transforming healthcare delivery, promising to enhance diagnostic precision, personalize treatment strategies, and streamline clinical workflows. While clinical benefits are well-documented, economic implications require rigorous evaluation. This review evaluates the economic outcomes (cost-effectiveness, utility, budget impact) of clinical AI interventions across hospitals, primary care, and community-based systems.

## 2. Results
The systematic review identified 19 studies published between 2020 and 2024, spanning various clinical domains (e.g., oncology, cardiology, ophthalmology, infectious diseases) and conducted in North America, Europe, and Asia.

- **Methods**: Cost-effectiveness analysis (CEA, n=14), cost-utility analysis (CUA, n=4), cost-minimization analysis (CMA, n=2), and budget impact analysis (BIA, n=1).
- **Quality**: The included studies showed high reporting quality based on CHEERS 2022 and Drummond checklist assessments.

### Key Economic Themes
1. **Comparative Economic Outcomes**: AI interventions frequently achieved comparable clinical outcomes to standard care while improving QALYs and reducing costs.
2. **Key Cost Drivers and Sensitivity Analyses**: Principal drivers include technology acquisition costs, implementation/integration costs, maintenance, and indirect costs (e.g., productivity losses, complications). Robustness was typically tested via sensitivity analyses.
3. **Economic Impact and Implementation Considerations**: Results are highly context-dependent, varying by clinical specialty, healthcare setting, and regional cost structures.

## 3. Discussion
Clinical AI interventions are often cost-effective or cost-saving, though findings vary significantly by context. Methodological limitations persist:
- **Static Modeling**: Many studies use static models, potentially overestimating benefits by failing to account for AI's adaptive learning.
- **Incomplete Accounting**: Often omitted costs include upfront capital expenditures and ongoing maintenance fees.
- **Equity Gaps**: Limited disaggregation of data by demographic variables raises concerns regarding algorithmic bias and unequal distribution of benefits.

Future research should prioritize dynamic modeling, comprehensive cost inclusion, and explicit integration of equity metrics to ensure robust guidance for policy and practice.

## Applications of Deep Learning Models in Laparoscopy for Gynecology

## Abstract
Background and Objectives: The use of Artificial Intelligence (AI) in the medical field is rapidly expanding. This review aims to explore and summarize all published research on the development and validation of deep learning (DL) models in gynecologic laparoscopic surgeries.

Materials and Methods: MEDLINE, IEEE Xplore, and Google scholar were searched for eligible studies published between January 2000 and May 2025. Selected studies developed a DL model using datasets derived from gynecologic laparoscopic procedures. The exclusion criteria included non-gynecologic datasets, non-laparoscopic datasets, non-Convolutional Neural Network (CNN) models, and non-English publications.

Results: A total of 16 out of 621 studies met our inclusion criteria. The findings were categorized into four main application areas: (i) anatomy classification (n = 6), (ii) anatomy segmentation (n = 5), (iii) surgical instrument classification and segmentation (n = 5), and (iv) surgical action recognition (n = 5).

Conclusions: This review emphasizes the growing role of AI in gynecologic laparoscopy, improving anatomy recognition, instrument tracking, and surgical action analysis. As datasets grow and computational capabilities advance, these technologies are poised to improve intraoperative guidance and standardize surgical training.

**Keywords**: laparoscopy, deep learning, convolutional neural network, artificial intelligence

---

## 1. Introduction
Minimally Invasive Surgery (MIS) is now the standard approach for numerous gynecologic procedures, offering advantages like decreased blood loss and reduced recovery time. However, the technical demands of laparoscopy pose challenges for inexperienced surgeons. Deep learning (DL), specifically Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs), offers potential for intraoperative support. This review evaluates CNN applications in gynecologic laparoscopy, including tissue classification, segmentation, and surgical phase recognition.

## 2. Materials and Methods
The review involved a comprehensive search of MEDLINE, IEEE Xplore, and Google Scholar for studies (2000-2025). Inclusion criteria focused on CNN-based models applied to gynecologic laparoscopic surgery, covering anatomy classification/segmentation, instrument detection, and surgical action recognition.

## 3. Results
A total of 16 studies met the criteria. Findings are categorized as follows:
- **3.1 Anatomy Classification**: Models used CNNs (GoogleNet, AlexNet, FrCNN, ResNet50, eENet) to classify organs or identify endometriotic lesions.
- **3.2 Anatomy Segmentation**: U-Net and Mask R-CNN architectures were used to segment uterus, ovaries, ureters, and uterine arteries.
- **3.3 Surgical Instruments**: Mask R-CNN and GoogleNet were applied for instrument segmentation and classification.
- **3.4 Surgical Action Recognition**: CNN, CNN-RNN, and other DL methods were used to classify surgical actions.

## 4. Discussion
DL models have shown promise in identifying anatomical structures and surgical instruments, with potential for real-time intraoperative guidance and objective assessment of surgical skills. Challenges remain, including the need for larger, high-quality, multi-institutional datasets and the standardization of evaluation metrics.

## 5. Conclusions
AI models show promise in gynecologic laparoscopy, with applications ranging from foundational to complex tasks. Future progress depends on high-quality datasets and rigorous clinical trial evaluation.

## Deep learning–driven multi-omics analysis: enhancing cancer diagnostics and therapeutics

## Abstract
Artificial intelligence (AI) excels at efficiently processing large volumes of data and extracting valuable insights. Deep Learning (DL), a subfield of AI, utilizes multi-layer neural network algorithms to analyze various types of data, mimicking the neural network architecture of the human brain. One of the most prominent features of DL is its end-to-end learning mechanism, which excels at automatic feature extraction and pattern recognition in data. As multi-omics technologies rapidly evolve, the volume of omics data from cancer samples has surged, presenting a significant challenge in managing this vast amount of information. Due to its strong data processing capabilities, DL is increasingly applied across a range of cancer research areas, such as early detection and screening, diagnosis, molecular subtype classification, discovery of biomarkers, and predicting patient prognosis and treatment responses. DL integrates high-dimensional data from fields such as genomics, epigenomics, transcriptomics, proteomics, radiomics, and single-cell omics, enhancing our understanding of cancer development and advancing personalized treatment approaches. This paper reviews various DL models and their roles in analyzing complex data patterns, providing a review of DL applications in cancer multi-omics analysis research and emphasizing its potential in early detection, diagnosis, classification, and prognosis prediction. As DL models are introduced continuously, we expect their application in cancer research to become more extensive, thus propelling the advancement of cancer medicine.

## Introduction
Artificial intelligence (AI) received its formal designation and definition at the 1956 Dartmouth Conference, signifying the formal inception of the AI discipline [1]. A subset of AI, machine learning (ML) is typically divided into supervised learning, unsupervised learning, and reinforcement learning. The essence involves utilizing algorithms to allow machines to autonomously learn from data and discover patterns, thus enabling decision-making or predictions. Deep learning (DL), a subset of ML, has existed in theory and algorithm since the 1980s, but it began to gain significant attention in 2006, driven by advancements in computational power and the availability of large datasets. DL employs multi-layer neural networks to mimic the way humans learn, enhancing prediction accuracy by learning the multi-layered representations of data. It has achieved remarkable success in fields like computer vision, natural language processing, and intelligent construction [2, 3].

The distinction between DL models and existing technologies primarily lies in the following aspects: feature extraction techniques, the requirements for data and computational resources, model interpretability, and approaches to solving problems. In terms of feature extraction methods, traditional techniques such as conventional computer vision methods require manual feature extraction. As the number of classification categories increases, feature extraction becomes increasingly cumbersome [4]. In contrast, DL models can automatically extract features without the need for manual selection of important features [3]. Regarding data and computational resource demands, DL requires access to large-scale datasets to surpass other methods, and training DL models necessitates high-performance computing units, robust Graphics Processing Units (GPUs), and significant storage capacity [5, 6]. In comparison, traditional technologies have relatively lower demands for data volume and computational resources. In terms of model interpretability, DL models are frequently criticized as “black boxes” due to their millions of parameters, each with intricate interrelations, making it very challenging to manually adjust the model parameters [7]. On the other hand, traditional techniques, such as conventional computer vision, offer full transparency. Engineers can assess whether a solution will work outside the training environment and can incorporate insights into the algorithm based on the problem [4, 6]. Regarding problem-solving approaches, traditional techniques like conventional computational imaging depend on physical models, limited by optical systems and imaging conditions. On the other hand, DL has revolutionized this “physics model-driven” paradigm, ushering in a “sample data-driven” approach to learning [8, 9]. Over the past few years, DL technology has demonstrated unique benefits in medical practice and has become an essential tool in oncology research.

The development and progression of cancer involves interactions across various levels such as the genome, epigenome, transcriptome, proteome, and metabolome. The integration of multi-omics data into cancer research assists us to improve a better understanding of the intrinsic framework of tumor development and lays the foundation for cancer diagnosis and personalized treatment. With the increasing volume of omics data, effectively integrating and analyzing these data amid noise and uncovering the potential information within vast amounts of data have become pressing challenges. DL, with its ability to handle high-dimensional, heterogeneous, and unstructured data, as well as its capability to discover complex relationships in multimodal data, offers a potential solution to this challenge [10, 11]. In recent years, DL technology has demonstrated its application potential in various stages of cancer management, including early detection and screening, diagnosis, molecular subtype classification, prognosis prediction, survival analysis, and treatment response assessment [12].

This paper reviews recent applications of multi-omics analysis combined with DL technology in cancer diagnosis and treatment. We present the basic concepts, workflows, and common strategies for DL data integration, and provide examples of several typical DL models. In addition, we discuss in detail the classification of multi-omics, the main data types in each omics, and the analysis techniques involved. Furthermore, we discuss the fundamental concepts of DL, various common DL models, the classification of multi-omics, and the foundational process of integrating multi-omics data using DL. Finally, we explore the specific uses of DL in the field of oncology, encompassing early detection and screening of cancer, diagnosis, subtype classification, discovery of biomarkers, and prognosis and treatment response prediction. Additionally, we summarize the current challenges and future outlook of DL technology.

## An overview of deep learning
### Introduction to deep learning
AI represents a cutting-edge technological advancement, possessing the ability to acquire knowledge and discern connections within datasets. It can effectively utilize this information to compute and produce results from new input data [13]. AI can process large-scale data efficiently and extract valuable information, patterns, and structures from it as well. ML is a subset of AI focused on data analysis, pattern recognition, and the construction of predictive models. DL, a subfield of ML (Fig. 1), draws inspiration from brain neural structures and utilizes multi-layer neural network algorithms to analyze various types of data and produce predictive outcomes [14]. The most prominent feature of DL is its end-to-end learning mechanism, which enables models to learn from raw data to final outcomes, bypassing manual feature engineering or intermediate processing steps. This characteristic not only conserves time in data analysis but also diminishes subjective errors in DL when dealing with complex issues, enhancing the efficiency and accuracy of the results [15]. Potential systemic errors, such as issues with data selection during the model training phase, can be mitigated by optimizing network architecture and selecting training samples to reduce their impact on model performance.

## Basic concepts, workflow, and common strategies of multi-omics data integration based on Deep Learning
### Basic concepts of deep learning
DL models, central to data integration, typically consist of several core components: input layer, hidden layers, output layer, pooling layers, weights and biases, activation functions, loss functions, optimizers, and regularization.

### Workflow of data integration
The workflow for multi-omics data integration using DL mainly involves six key stages: data preprocessing, feature selection or dimensionality reduction, data integration, DL model construction, data analysis, and result validation (Fig. 2).

### Common strategies for multi-omics integration
The core challenge of multi-omics data integration lies in how to effectively merge different omics data. These data vary in scale, resolution, and nature, and direct integration can result in data mismatches, noise interference, and scale inconsistencies.

## Interpretability of deep learning
DL models are typically seen as “black box,” making it challenging to clearly elucidate their reasoning or decision-making processes, thus having low interpretability. As computing power increases, DL models become more complex and more difficult to explain.

## Various deep learning neural network models
The following introduces several DL neural network models that can be used for multi-omics data integration:
*   **Multilayer perceptron (MLP)**
*   **Convolutional Neural Network (CNN)**
*   **Recurrent Neural Network (RNN) and Long Short-Term Memory Network (LSTM)**
*   **Autoencoder (AE)**
*   **Graph Convolutional Network (GCN)**
*   **Transformer architecture**

## Multi-omics data and technologies
Multi-omics integrates data from genomics, transcriptomics, proteomics, radiomics, and single-cell omics, describing nearly all biomolecules from genes to metabolites, enabling a more comprehensive and systematic understanding of biological systems’ complexity (Fig. 3).

### Genomics
Originating from the Human Genome Project, genomics involves studying the structure, function, evolution, and environmental interactions of an organism’s genome [66].

### Epigenomics
The main data types involved in epigenomics include: DNA methylation data, histone modification data, chromatin conformation data, and chromatin accessibility data [75].

### Transcriptomics
Transcriptomics involves the analysis of the entire set of RNA transcripts produced in an organism, capturing their expression profiles under specific conditions.


---

## Integration of Artificial Intelligence-Enhanced Capsule Endoscopy in Clinical Practice: A Review of Market-Available Tools for Clinical Practice

## Abstract
The integration of artificial intelligence (AI) into capsule endoscopy is transforming gastrointestinal diagnostics by enhancing lesion detection and optimizing reading efficiency. This review focuses on the clinical applications of commercially available AI-powered capsule endoscopy systems, particularly for small bowel evaluation. Recent clinical trials and observational studies are analyzed to assess the diagnostic performance, practical benefits, and limitations of these systems. Additionally, key challenges related to standardization, data quality, and clinical validation are discussed. Currently available AI systems significantly reduce reading times and demonstrate high detection capabilities, depending on the algorithm and device used. However, a substantial number of lesions remain undetected, preventing full reliance on these tools. Future advancements must focus on improving detection rates and validating the clinical relevance of missed lesions. Additionally, standardizing AI algorithms across different capsule systems is essential to ensure consistency, reliability, and broader clinical adoption. Establishing homologation frameworks will be key to achieving uniform performance and seamless integration into routine practice.

**Keywords:** Intelligence, Artificial, Capsule, Endoscopy, Small bowel, Crohn’s disease, Bleeding

## Introduction
Capsule endoscopy (CE) is a widely used technique that has become an essential component of the modern endoscopic arsenal for gastroenterologists. As a digital imaging modality, artificial intelligence (AI) can be directly integrated into CE workstations to enhance its diagnostic capabilities.

CE is the first-line diagnostic tool for evaluating small bowel bleeding, offering a minimally invasive endoscopic approach to intestinal assessment. Beyond this primary application, CE has been extensively studied for colon examination and pan-enteric evaluation. However, technological limitations continue to restrict its routine use in the upper gastrointestinal tract, particularly from the mouth to the duodenal bulb.

Despite its advantages, CE has two major limitations that hinder broader adoption: the need for specialized training and prolonged reading times. Interpretation requires proficiency in image analysis and lesion detection, with a well-defined learning curve. Reading times typically range from 30 to 60 min, depending on the reader’s expertise and familiarity with image interpretation. 

The two most significant indications for CE are the evaluation of suspected small bowel bleeding (SSBB) and the assessment of suspected or confirmed small bowel Crohn’s disease (CD). Given its reliance on software-based image processing, CE represents an ideal platform for AI integration, with the potential to reduce reading times and enhance diagnostic accuracy.

This review provides a clinically oriented synthesis of AI integration in CE, with a focus on systems currently available for routine clinical use.

## Artificial Intelligence: Role in Endoscopy and Capsule Endoscopy
AI is broadly defined as an automated system capable of mimicking human cognitive abilities. While fully automated AI systems are well established in other fields of modern engineering, applications in gastrointestinal (GI) endoscopy primarily focus on image recognition.

AI algorithms for image analysis in endoscopy are generally classified into two main categories: computer-aided detection (CADe), which assists in identifying lesions, and computer-aided diagnosis (CADx), which aids in lesion characterization and classification. Modern deep learning algorithms, particularly convolutional neural networks (CNNs), have revolutionized endoscopic AI by enabling automatic feature extraction and superior pattern recognition.

The European Society of Gastrointestinal Endoscopy (ESGE) has established criteria for AI implementation in GI endoscopy, emphasizing that AI systems should perform at least as well as experienced endoscopists. In small bowel capsule endoscopy (SBCE), AI should enhance lesion detection while reducing video assessment time.

## Clinical Indications for Market-Available Capsule Endoscopy
Currently, two clinically established CE devices are widely adopted in clinical practice: SBCE and colon capsule endoscopy (CCE). SBCE is primarily indicated for the evaluation of SSBB, iron deficiency anemia, CD, Peutz-Jeghers syndrome, small bowel tumors, and refractory or equivocal cases of celiac disease.

CCE is primarily used for colon evaluation in average-risk populations and as an alternative for high-risk patients when conventional colonoscopy is contraindicated. A potentially more practical application of CCE is its use in low-risk populations with non-specific symptoms. Additionally, pan-enteric capsule endoscopy (PCE) is also possible with this type of capsule, allowing for both small bowel and colon assessment.

Crohn’s disease (CD) is a key area of CE application, and ongoing research aims to expand its role. Recently, a modified capsule with dual cameras, derived from the PillCam™ Colon Capsule 2, known as PillCam™ Crohn’s Capsule (PCC), has been introduced, enabling PCE in a single procedure.

## Endoscopic Capsules with Integrated AI Systems
Several commercially available CE systems have incorporated AI to assist in small bowel evaluation.

- **Medtronic plc (USA):** The PillCam™ SB system includes tools like TOP100, an AI-powered proprietary tool designed to extract and display frames with the highest probability of containing significant findings.
- **IntroMedic (South Korea):** The MiroCam® MC1600 integrates the Express View reading system, which uses traditional machine learning to recognize basic image features and create a condensed video.
- **Junshan Science & Technology (China):** The OMOM® HD integrates a CNN-based CADe algorithm for lesion identification and redundant frame deletion, using the SmartScan tool.
- **Ankon/AnX Robotica (China/USA):** The Navicam® SB incorporates a deep CNN algorithm within its reading software, known as ProScan, which generates a shortened video with findings highlighted.

## AI in Capsule Endoscopy for Suspected Small Bowel Bleeding
SSBB is the most common indication for SBCE. While numerous AI algorithms are being developed, only a few studies have evaluated already market-available AI applications for SSBB. Studies have shown that AI-assisted reading significantly reduces interpretation time, often with maintained or improved sensitivity compared to standard reading. However, suboptimal negative predictive values and miss rates mean it cannot yet fully replace expert review.

## AI in Capsule Endoscopy for Small Bowel Crohn’s Disease
While AI has been extensively studied in SSBB, its application in CD remains relatively unexplored. Some studies have shown that AI-assisted detection of CD ulcers can achieve high sensitivity, and AI-driven automated scoring systems have shown strong correlation with established indices like the Lewis Score. However, further prospective studies are required.

## AI in Capsule Endoscopy for Other Clinical Applications
CCE represents another promising area for AI application, particularly in reducing unnecessary invasive procedures. AI may also play a crucial role in bowel preparation assessment, which directly influences the reliability of CE interpretations.

## Emerging Experimental AI Models in Capsule Endoscopy
Experimental research has advanced rapidly, exploring novel approaches beyond current market-available AI algorithms. Deep learning architectures, particularly CNNs, have demonstrated superior performance in lesion detection in research settings. There is growing interest in multi-lesion detection systems that better reflect the diagnostic complexity of CE.

## Ethical Dilemmas with AI in Capsule Endoscopy
There are currently no globally standardized frameworks for training and validation of AI algorithms in capsule endoscopy. Significant concerns include:
- Bias in AI models, potentially leading to exclusion of certain groups.
- The "black box" problem: lack of transparency in AI decision-making.
- Accountability and liability when AI produces incorrect or ambiguous predictions.

## Limitations and Future Innovations of AI and CE
The main limitation of AI models in CE remains their miss rate. Future research should focus on enhancing AI sensitivity and specificity through integration of advanced image enhancement, more diverse training datasets, and multimodal data fusion. An upcoming innovation is real-time AI integration within the capsule itself. Moreover, future AI-driven platforms may incorporate remote reading, automated triage, and telemedicine.

## Conclusions
Despite the growing number of experimental algorithms, evidence on the practical application of AI in commercially available CE systems remains limited. Current AI tools primarily function as triage systems. The future of AI in CE will depend on demonstrating that increased speed and autonomy in identifying lesions can lead to improved patient outcomes.

## A roadmap of artificial intelligence applications in pediatric surgery: a comprehensive review of applications, challenges, and ethical considerations

## Abstract

Artificial intelligence (AI) and machine learning (ML) are rapidly transforming healthcare, with growing interest in their application to rare pediatric surgical conditions. In these settings, limited data availability often brakes traditional research. Although pediatric surgery has historically been slower than other specialties in adopting ML, recent years have seen an increase in AI-driven tools designed for surgical care. This review presents an overview of AI applications in pediatric surgery, highlighting current uses, benefits, challenges, and their potential clinical impact. A comprehensive literature search was conducted to identify studies on AI and ML models relevant to pediatric surgery. The findings indicate that ML is mainly applied in predictive decision support, particularly for preoperative risk stratification, intraoperative navigation, and postoperative outcome prediction. AI is especially valuable in endoscopic and minimally invasive procedures, where it enhances the visualization of anatomical landmarks. In pediatric oncologic surgery, AI aids in the accurate localization and delineation of tumors. Additionally, AI improves pathology workflows through automated image analysis and annotation, supporting both diagnosis and education. Despite these advances, ethical and regulatory challenges remain. Ensuring data privacy and obtaining informed consent are essential. When responsibly implemented, AI can significantly improve pediatric surgical care.

## Introduction

Artificial intelligence (AI) and machine learning technologies are rapidly transforming nearly all fields, including health-care. The entire medical scientific community is fascinated by the opportunity to apply these algorithms in clinical practice, especially in addressing rare conditions to overcome the scarcity of data in existing series, facilitating a more robust and supported diagnostic and therapeutic process [1]. Although some sub-fields of medicine, such as pediatric surgery, have been relatively slow to obtain the critical benefits of deep learning, related research in this field is begging to accumulate significantly [2]. Hence, in this paper, we examined recent AI applications relevant to pediatric surgery. This review aimed first to provide an overview of current AI implementations, highlighting specific machine learning/AI limitations in pediatric surgery field and second to categorize pediatric surgery-related AI applications into macrodomains, to explain their sub-domains and the important elements of the applicable AI models discussing how these technologies will advance the field of pediatric care.

## Methods

PubMed was searched from database inception to January 15 2025, for articles addressing AI in pediatric surgery. To guide our search, the search terms of “pediatric surgery”, “artificial intelligence”, “machine learning”, “deep-learning”, “computer vision” and “natural language processing” were used combined with Boolean operators “AND”, “OR”. Articles written in languages other than English and articles in the form of conference abstracts, editorials, and commentaries were excluded. There was no further limitation on study design. After the selection of studies, data from each article were extracted and organised into 3 main subset of AI (Machine learning, computer vision, Natural language processing) in a standardised data extraction form developed in Microsoft Excel. This was done independently by two authors to record the information and synthesise it in summary format (M.D, A.B). Extracted information included author name(s), year of publication, title, location of study, study design, goal of study, target study population, description of discussed AI intervention, domain of AI used, data source, evaluations of AI tool accuracy/efficacy, main results of the study, identified barriers to clinical integration of described application were extracted.

## Results

Our comprehensive review identified a wide range of artificial intelligence (AI) subsets currently explored within pediatric surgery, each offering unique strengths and applications. Among these, the most prominent are Machine Learning (ML), Computer Vision, and Natural Language Processing (NLP).

### Machine learning

Machine learning, a subset of AI, is a scientific discipline focused on understanding how computers can learn from data. It is broadly defined as the computational ability to learn from experience, recognize patterns, and make predictions. In recent years, ML has emerged as a cornerstone of AI applications in medicine, including pediatric surgery, where it plays a key role in improving diagnosis, optimizing treatment decisions, and predicting outcomes [3]. ML encompasses a variety of algorithms designed to classify data accurately or make precise predictions based on the analysis of structured and unstructured data. It is generally categorized into three main types: supervised, unsupervised and reinforcement learning (Table 1). In pediatric surgery, ML is most commonly used to develop tailored clinical decision support systems and predictive models for surgical outcomes, complications, and patient survival.

| Definition of machine learning approach | |
| :--- | :--- |
| Supervised learning | Learns from labeled medical data with known outcomes (for instance Reisman et al. [7]) |
| Unsupervised learning | Discovers patterns in unlabeled medical data without predefined targets. (for instance, Sylvester et al. [18]) |
| Reinforcement learning | Learns to make sequential decisions by interacting with an environment and improving based on feedback |

### Clinical Decision Support Systems (CDSS)

We consider the most common area where ML has been applied in the pediatric surgery filed.

#### Appendicitis diagnosis and management

Recent advancements in ML have significantly improved the diagnosis and management of appendicitis, the most common emergency in pediatric surgery. The studies used a variety of ML approaches, considering data from clinical evaluations, laboratory measurements, and diagnostic imaging to create and assess clinical diagnostic tools. AI solutions offer similar sensitivity and superior specificity to existing scoring systems, like idelberg Appendicitis Score (HAS), Pediatric Appendicitis Score (PAS), Alvarado-Score, and Tzanakis-Score. AI tools can integrate multifaceted data inputs to provide more precise risk stratification and diagnostic predictions [4, 5]. Specifically Aydin et al. developed a decision tree model based on clinical, laboratory, and radiological findings, which achieved a 94% AUC and 95% accuracy for appendicitis diagnosis. However, the model's performance dropped when distinguishing complicated cases (AUC 79%, accuracy 70%) [6]. Similarly, Reisman et al. applied supervised learning, incorporating ultrasound-based appendix diameter, achieving AUCs of 0.91 to diagnose appendicitis [7]. Marcinkevics et al. based on a range of information encompassing history, clinical examination, laboratory parameters, and abdominal ultrasonography, compared multiple ML models and identified random forests as superior, with AUCs of 0.94 (diagnosis), 0.92 (management), and 0.70 (severity) [8]. Their findings led to the development of a user-friendly online prediction tool. Despite the current lack of generalizability, each of the studies reviewed showed encouraging findings for the use of AI to diagnose appendicitis in pediatric population.

#### Hirschsprung disease (HD)

ML techniques have shown significant promise in improving the diagnosis of HD. Traditional diagnostic methods, such as contrast enemas and histological assessment of rectal biopsies, can be subjective and require specialized expertise. ML-based approaches are helping to overcome these challenges. For instance, Huang et al. developed support vector machine (SVM) and logistic regression models that outperformed radiologists in identifying short-segment HD, achieving AUCs of 0.91 and 0.93, respectively [9]. In the histopathological domain, Duci et al. applied a U-Net convolutional neural network (CNN) to automatically detect ganglion cells and hypertrophic nerves on digital histology slides, reaching impressive accuracies of 92.3% and 91.5% [10]. Similarly, Greenberg et al. applied hierarchical contextual analysis (HCA) to histological data, achieving 96% sensitivity and 99% specificity for ganglion cell detection, with multi-center validation confirming 99.2% classification accuracy [11]. These advances in deep learning and feature-based classification have the potential to assist pathologists in challenging cases, reduce diagnostic delays, and standardize interpretation across centers. As these tools continue to evolve, they may be integrated into digital pathology workflows, especially in resource-limited settings, to support earlier and more reliable HD diagnosis.

#### Necrotizing enterocolitis (NEC)

NEC remains a life-threatening condition in neonates, and ML has been extensively used to identify early biomarkers and stratify disease severity. In a pioneering study, Mueller et al. used artificial neural networks (ANN) on 57 clinical variables and identified small for gestational age and mechanical ventilation as key predictors of NEC [12]. For biomarker discovery, Pantalone et al. employed random forest (RF) algorithms to analyze complete blood count (CBC) data at different time points before NEC onset, distinguishing effectively surgical NEC from controls but was less successful in differentiating surgical from medical NEC [13]. Six supervised ML models on 74 clinical variables were explored with logistic regression (LR) and RF as the best models identifying key predictors such as gestational age, birth weight, maternal chorioamnionitis, surfactant and patent ductus arteriosus therapy (medical or surgical ligation), as key differentiators for NEC prediction accuracy [14]. In addition, a recent model combined ResNet34 (for imaging) and one-dimensional CNN (for lab data) across 408 abdominal X-rays and 11,016 lab tests. The model achieved 94% accuracy and an AUC of 0.91, outperforming traditional methods and matching expert-level diagnostic accuracy [15]. Recent advancements have also explored the combination of ML techniques with non-invasive monitoring tools such as near-infrared spectroscopy (NIRS) to improve NEC risk assessment. Verhoeven et al. integrated ML with NIRS data to detect low abdominal oxygen saturation (ArSO₂ < 50%) within 24 h of life—linked to a higher NEC incidence. ML models analyzing continuous NIRS monitoring could aid in early NEC detection and intervention. ML algorithms have also demonstrated significant promise in stratifying NEC severity [16]. Ji et al. introduced an ML-based risk stratification model using linear discriminant analysis (LDA) on features such as pneumatosis intestinalis, portal venous gas, and metabolic acidosis at the onset of the disease [17]. Their model predicted NEC severity with an AUC of 0.85 and showed strong agreement with manual staging. Sylvester et al. combined urine peptide profiling with unsupervised ML to distinguish surgical from medical NEC [18]. Their integrated LDA model outperformed clinical-only models in stratifying NEC severity. More recently, a novel Ridge Regression and Q-learning-based Bee Swarm Optimization (RQBSO) algorithm, a hybrid approach offering robust optimization capabilities in AI-driven healthcare, was applied to identify key clinical and laboratory parameters as significant indicators of severe NEC requiring surgery. Their model identified key features like anemia, high WBC, peritoneal signs, and early onset as severe predictive factors achieving 91.88% AUROC [19].

### Pediatric urology

ML is increasingly being applied in pediatric urology to enhance diagnostic precision and support clinical decision-making, particularly in conditions where interpretation of imaging or anatomical classification can be complex. Blum et al. developed a supervised learning algorithm that analyzed features extracted from diuresis renograms to identify ureteropelvic junction obstruction (UPJO), achieving an impressive area under the curve (AUC) of 0.96. This approach demonstrates the potential for ML to reduce diagnostic subjectivity and streamline the evaluation of functional uropathies [20]. Further extending ML applications to urinary tract conditions, Babajide et al. repurposed a brain MRI segmentation deep learning model to accurately characterize urinary stones on CT in both adult and pediatric patients, achieving high sensitivity and specificity while outperforming radiologists in measurement efficiency [21].
